Katalog Berita (Halaman 43/57)

#2984. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

Disclosures: Tidal owns/holds all the securities mentioned in the article.

#2979. Phoenix is falling behind, and there’s no room for another slow start

There are plenty of things in life that give people anxiety. For me, it's being late. Or even the threat of being late. Perhaps it's the military man in me, somebody who made the mistake of showing up late to formation during basic training and paid for it in the front-leaning rest position, beads of sweat dripping off my nose and forming a puddle beneath me on the warm pavement at Fort Leonard Wood, Missouri, one June morning. If you're not early, you're late. That's been my mantra ever since.

Unfortunately, that familiar anxiety is beginning to build regarding the Phoenix Suns. This team is 12th in the Western Conference standings after starting 1-4, something we've seen before. At this point last season, the Suns were also 1-4 and were 13th. The difference then is they were only 3.0 games out of first, and due to an odd scheduling start for the Suns and the fact that they have played the fewest games in the NBA, they are 4.5 games back of the top team in the league. That team? The Rockets…

These slow starts create a significant barrier between the Suns and a proverbial train that's already leaving the station, headed toward the postseason. That's right, the Suns are in danger of being late for that train. Anxiety! Because with every passing day and every loss that accumulates, they find themselves falling further and further behind, making it increasingly difficult to hop on board and reach their desired postseason destination.

A season ago, we found ourselves in the same predicament, but the feeling surrounding the team was much different. A big part of that came down to expectations. Nobody really expected the Suns to be a team that could compete at a high level. The prevailing thought was to let the season play out, sell off assets around the trade deadline, and begin building toward the summer of 2026.

Following that 1-4 start, the Suns began putting things together. The sloppy basketball we'd witnessed early in the season became more organized, and the effort that was evident through those first five games finally started translating into wins. I had this to say after they lost 114-113 to the Grizzlies a season ago:

Losing still stings, but this team isn't miles away. The execution needs tightening, and these are the aches that come with learning how to win again.

It's tough to find moral victories in Game 5 when you're sitting at 1–4, but I'll say it anyway. I was entertained. Disappointed, sure. The ending wasn't what anyone wanted. But maybe this year isn't about wins and losses. Maybe it's about how they respond to nights like this.

The feeling this year is different, however. Locally, expectations were higher, even if the national perception remained relatively unchanged. The roster construction was clunky, but you could at least see the vision of what this team wanted to become. Regardless, those expectations change the reality of your situation. While the effort has certainly been present, its consistency has begun to wane, and the execution remains frustratingly inconsistent.

Last season, we could talk ourselves into believing thatDillon Brooks and Jalen Green's absence due to injury was a significant reason for the team's struggles. And perhaps this year, we're doing the same thing with Mark Williams. But the feeling isn't the same. The reality of the situation is slowly sinking in, and it's one in which hope is becoming increasingly difficult to find. This is a team that doesn't control its own draft picks, so finding itself stuck in the middle of the standings is essentially a death sentence.

When you consider the anti-tanking rules that will be in place this year, there's a very real possibility that the Suns could find themselves among the teams with an 8.1% chance of landing the number one overall pick. And how "Suns" would it be for Phoenix to benefit from not being completely horrendous under the league's new draft lottery structure, only to have to hand that pick over to Houston?

Are we there yet? Are we seriously thinking about this already? (I am) When you start 1-4 and look around the standings at the other teams with only one win, seeing the Kings, Nets, Jazz, Bulls, and Bucks alongside you, it's difficult not to get a little frazzled. Because those are the teams that were projected to be bad. It's why we're running to the trade machine trying to find fixes, because you have no room for a down season when there is no benefit to being bad.

I think back to a season ago and the turnaround we witnessed. What the Suns accomplished was impressive, climbing out of 13th place in the Western Conference and playing some incredibly good basketball along the way. For one fleeting night on January 22, 2026, the Suns had fought their way all the way back to the fifth seed in the Western Conference. After starting 1-4, they went 26-13 to reach that point. That is going to be incredibly hard to replicate.

Can this team reproduce any aspect of what we witnessed last year? Can they put together another impressive run that allows them to hop aboard that train headed toward the postseason?

There is hope if you look hard enough. Through their first 5 games a season ago, the Suns had a 120.5 defensive rating (28th) and -9.8 net rating (27th). This season, they are at 124.0 (30th) and -9.8 (25th). After their 1-4 start a season ago, the Suns posted a 112.3 defensive rating (8th) and a +2.1 net rating (13th). It is possible. We've seen it before.

Psst…I'm saying the quiet part out loud…that team can rebound the damn ball…the worst team in rebound percentage last season was the Washington Wizards at 47.1%. The Suns are currently at 45.7%…

We've got a long trip ahead of us, my fellow Suns fans, and we'll spend much of that journey wondering whether this team can recapture any semblance of what it was a season ago. We'll look for solutions in every corner, talking ourselves into the viability of overpaid players like Domantas Sabonis or Dejounte Murray as mediocre saviors. But with so many red flags surrounding the roster construction, it's difficult to shake that familiar feeling of anxiety from being late building in the pit of your stomach.

#2958. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2950. What We Learned from the Spurs win over the Jazz

Somewhere in the second quarter, with the Spurs up one and about to be up more, I realized I wasn't watching the game. I was, instead, watching myself not watch the game, which is a worse hobby and the one I'm apparently best at. The game was fine. The game was exactly what it said on the label: a nondescript tilt between two teams already headed down different tracks. The shine of October basketball had worn off completely and, like summer turning to fall, the Grind had arrived.

As a first quarter blowout turned into a 2nd quarter snoozer, my brain began to wander a bit. I could be anywhere in the world right now. A plastic stool in Hanoi, a bowl of pho, a little light rain. A mountain pass in the Andes, thin air, nobody asking me for a take. Man, I should be reading books. Seeing films. I should be the kind of person whose passport has all those cool stamps in it. This one precious life the universe handed me was unraveling in real time as Ace Bailey stepped into a 25-foot pull-up three to cut the Jazz's deficit to one.

Then Stephon Castle missed a layup and I almost decided to call it a night. It was as annoying as it was unremarkable and I just didn't need it anymore. I reached for the remote and, as I did, Victor Wembanyama, who a minute earlier had similarly flubbed an alley-oop, grabbed the rebound and tipped it back in. Twenty seconds after Bailey's three. No celebration. Barely a facial expression.

I'd love to tell you that's the moment I'd been looking for, but that would be too tidy. What I'd been searching for was a hook. A story, a cause, a catalyst. Anything to hold off the particular ennui of being a fan of a good team. And Wemby wasn't even good last night, not by any stretch. Five of fifteen. Zero for five from three. Five turnovers. He was in that mode he gets in from time to time where he decides the Key to Victory is to simply chuck it.

The Spurs won by 22 anyway, not because of a miracle but because this roster has more than one way to be competent. The Spurs are, mercifully, more than the sum of their parts.

Not that the parts are bad. Quite the contrary. Dylan Harper scored all 17 of his points before the third quarter was half over. Stephon Castle is an animal. De'Aaron Fox looks like the guy we paid for. Tarris Reed continues to grab rebounds in a way that feels like it's going to be important (seven in about seven minutes last night). Every wing combination we've thrown on the court is its own kind of fun.

I mean, look at Harrison Barnes. A man as old as time itself (he's four years younger than me) had an insanely active night. He knocked down threes from 29, 25 and 24 feet, he ran around, he caused trouble. Early in the fourth he got tangled up with Brice Sensabaugh, slid across the floor, got back up, and just stared at him while the refs reviewed it in slow motion. I'm told that clip racked up 140,000 views on the world wide web within about an hour.

The thing about the Grind is that it doesn't have to mean anything, which means that everything within the Grind gets to be exactly what it is. A tip dunk. A well executed outlet pass. An old man staring down Brice Sensabaugh. Like, I didn't come out of the womb craving the Larry O'Brien trophy. I just was at my breakfast table in 1996 watching Michael Jordan dunk on Rik Smits during a SportsCenter rerun, and some part of me said, with zero strategic vision, "Yes. More of that." More of that was the entire ask and, frankly, it still is.

Look at us. You and me. Like I said, we could be anywhere in the world right now, but we're right here with the Spurs and the Jazz on a Monday night in November. The stools in Hanoi will still be there someday, but a precocious superstar, having the worst night of his season, is putting a ball back through the hoop like a child tucking in his stuffed animals for the night.

You don't have to impress some greater importance on a moment like that to make it mean something. You were there. It's a part of you now and you got to live it.

If that isn't special, I don't know what is.

Dylan Harper seems almost casually good at basketball. It's remarkable and breathtaking, these things he's doing, but it also has that effect of seeming effortless when he's casually dismantling another team. In the second quarter he stole the ball and in a blink Vassell was dunking off his pass. In the third, he stole it again and Stephon was finishing an alley-oop before I'd even registered it. He added 8 assists and 7 rebounds on top of all his scoring. I find myself almost taking it for granted at this point, and we've really only started to scratch the surface of what he's capable of. Scary to think about! (For our opponents. For us it's just exceedingly cool and fun.)

I know it's just the Jazz, grain of salt and all that, but I never get tired of seeing the Spurs get out on the break and run. They won fast-break points 26-9, against a Jazz team that turned it over just 11 times, a season low for Utah, so a lot of that was pure rebound-and-go. They also won the paint 52-36 and opened the game on an 18-2 run. Run for days my boys. Grab the ball and never stop running. Only good can come from this. Open looks from three, easy paths to the rim, potential foul trouble for the opponents. Push that pace. Push that pace. PUSH THAT PACE.

This is not how development works and, to be clear, I don't know what I'm talking about, but I absolutely crave Tarris Reed minutes. I want him to become a Sasquatch, Godzilla, King Kong, Loch Ness, Goblin, ghoul and a zombie with no conscience. I want basketballs to fear him checking into the game because they're about to get rebounded to within an inch of their lives.

I love a wire-to-wire win. Next time you hear me complaing about a wire to wire win feel free to come to my house and take an ice cold waterloo out of my fridge as payment for my sins.

I'm a man of all seasons, to be clear, but right now, yes, I am currently in a Waterloo phase of my Sparkling Water career.

There is a particular Black Cherry flavor I've grown fond of and I just can't stay away at this point. It scratches this weird nostalgic part of my brain that yearns for a Blue Sky soda I had in my youth. Do you remember Blue Sky soda?

Yeah, the cans were cool. I don't know. I think I had just never experienced the concept of a soda that existed outside the mainstream world of the Coca-Cola and Pepsi family trees, so when I went on vacation and saw this crazy looking can with a cherry sitting on a cloud it just… broke my brain a little bit and I had to have it.

Who could say? I was six years old. I'd left the summer heat in Texas and woken up the next day in a Colorado mountain town sipping an alien soda from heaven. Maybe it was good, maybe it wasn't, but it stirred something deep in my soul that I think I've been searching for ever since.

And Black Cherry Waterloos scratch that itch for you?

They do. Publix also pretty regularly throws them in Buy One Get Ones for me and who am I to turn down a deal like that.

#2946. Sullivan: What the 2026 midterms could mean for energy investors

Don't expect huge near-term moves in energy stocks off the results of the midterms. But there are some investing implications over the longer-term. Let me break it down.

Yes, it's entirely possible that one or both houses of Congress will shift to Democratic control. That's not my opinion. It's what many of the research reports, polls, and prediction markets are suggesting as of this writing.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

For argument's sake, let's say that this time the polls are correct. The election ends with a "blue trickle," and Democrats win out with a slight edge in the House, Senate, or both. It won't be enough to meaningfully change, well, anything. If the election ends with just a slight lean to the Democratic side, it won't be enough for a veto-proof majority. Candidates can specify policies — like halting the growth of data centers — all they want, but there's a chasm between being elected and passing new laws.

Research I've read and conversations we've had on CNBC suggest that the next two years in Washington, D.C., will be flush with finger-pointing, investigations into the president and his family, and posturing ahead of the far-more-important 2028 presidential election.

S&P Global doesn't exactly agree with me, saying that Democratic control of either chamber would bring "tougher oversight of agency decisions" and could impact things such as tax incentives and "other policies underpinning long-term energy investments."

A Democratic Congress could be good for offshore wind, an energy source the president seems to hate with the white-hot passion of the sun. Speaking of the sun, a shift blue could also be positive for the continued growth of solar, perhaps via new tax incentives. Though both are growing rapidly.

If the Republicans maintain control over one or both houses, we keep doing more of the same, albeit with more urgency. S&P Global adds that "a continued Republican majority would give the administration more room to pursue expanded domestic production, faster permitting and environmental deregulation, though narrow margins could limit major legislation."

Any change in control of Congress is also unlikely to make a big difference around the Iran conflict. Once any new Congress begins in January, things will have either ramped up, stayed the same, or cooled down. A new Congress could try to use its authority under the War Powers Act. The team at Piper Sandler agrees, writing that "elections matter, but will likely not change the direction of things" as Trump focuses more on his own legacy. That's a polite way of saying that the President is going to do largely what he wants, Congress be darned.

On the state level, the midterms may matter a little bit more, particularly as they relate to energy-hungry data centers. In Texas, a change in the statehouse could bring a change in sentiment around data centers. Candidate Gina Hinojosa has a policy to "end data center madness" and supports a moratorium on new builds. Amy Acton also supports a "conditional moratorium" on new data center builds in Ohio. Vivek Ramaswamy is also calling for conditions on new data center construction.

The issue is a big deal in many states. Yet even if those candidates, or candidates with similar views on big new energy projects, win, they'd still have to enact meaningful change. Which could happen, of course. So let's say it does happen. New governors enter office and use their power to throttle or even stop new projects. Even if some of the big new projects are stalled or stopped, power demand, at a minimum, would stay at current levels. Which means demand for power production inputs such as natural gas, nuclear, and yes, even coal, would remain at current levels.

As far as power-hungry utility stocks go, Bank of America agrees with my election take above - mostly. Its team writes that it thinks the "2026 cycle matters for utility equities in far fewer states than the headline count suggests," but that committee appointments in each state will matter. The BofA team says that, regarding the election and utility stocks, five states matter most: Georgia, Ohio, Oregon, Pennsylvania, and Texas.

Texas is the "most consequential," as state environmental regulators have halted data center permits. That state's Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) has actually stopped electronic permitting to make sure any permit is unrelated to data centers. To my point, that's already known information. It matters, but markets are already aware of it and have priced it in. Regardless, if you are investing in anything related to data centers, it wouldn't hurt to pay attention to Texas' election most of all.

There's a lot here. So take a breath, step back, and consider where we are right now.

The Strategic Petroleum Reserve is at a 40-year low.

We have two wars in some of the most important energy regions in the world.

The backlog for data centers - and thus the power needed to run them - is years long. Even if a number of them end up canceled, others will be built.

These days, it's hard to be certain of anything. But I am certain that overall energy demand isn't going down in America anytime soon. The only way to go is up. It's just a question of how much farther up.

That's my opinion. It could be wrong. And since I love hearing from you, I put out a poll on both X and LinkedIn. The results suggest you disagree with me.

Note → I am fully aware that the results of any poll on X or LinkedIn will be skewed by my type and makeup of followers on each platform - who lean heavily into both business, money and energy.

So if the elections won't matter in the near term for energy, what does?

Glad you asked.  Here's my ranking of what will matter for energy in the next year:

(Disagree?  Hit me up on X or LinkedIn and let me know why)

An end to either the Iran conflict or Russia's war on Ukraine would be massively positive for the world and send oil, natural gas, and refined products lower relatively quickly. Let's all hope for peace.

Lower interest rates would help many of the beaten-up stocks that rely on debt and consumer lending to prosper. Think residential solar panels.

The macro economy is going to do what it will, regardless of the U.S. elections, and American tax policy is largely unchangeable for the next couple of years.

Permitting reform is, of course, one factor that could be impacted by the election outcome, though it's unlikely to happen in the near term.

No, my friends, whatever the outcome of America's elections, it's safe to say the real political attention will be on Trump, more Trump, Trump's family, even more Trump, and then looking ahead to 2028's presidential vote.

If we could harness political infighting into some sort of power source, it may be better than nuclear!

I'm leaving for Europe this week to continue our five-year coverage of Europe's slow-rolling energy crisis. And while the word "crisis" is way overused in the media, I don't think it is here. Natural gas import prices are soaring. Storage levels are below recent averages. Interest rates are rising, and the public is already rage-filled about higher energy prices. It's a toxic combo. And I'll be in Europe later this week to talk about it.

First, from a macro markets perspective, I remain wary of tech and the stock market right now. As I posted on October 14, the markets "feel fragile."

Since writing that, the Nasdaq is down over 5%. A.I. isn't in a bubble, but not all companies are going to prosper. Some may go away. Watch the debt markets, particularly some A.I.-related high-yield (aka "junk") bonds. The State Street High Yield (JNK) ETF is prominently on my screens these days. Get to know the term "leveraged ETF," because those are in focus these days. The selling in many of these products can lead to selling in other products and stocks. If we get a slowdown in selling in those, some of the big tech stocks that have been selling off might stabilize.

Turning more specifically to why you are here: energy.

Earnings are (almost) behind us. ExxonMobil (XOM) and Chevron (CVX) posted big numbers last week. Marathon Petroleum (MPC) just reported, and those numbers were even bigger. The company earned $26.59 per share on $11.7 billion in EBITDA. That compares to EPS of $3.01 on earnings of $3.2 billion EBITDA one year ago.

ConocoPhillips (COP) caps things off later this week.

What now is that the world waits, watches, and focuses on Iran, the Strait of Hormuz, and Russia. What happens with those will determine the path of prices for oil and much more. Peace, and prices go down. New attacks, prices go higher. Higher prices mean many big-cap oil and gas stocks keep going higher. If Russia continues its brutal war against Ukraine, diesel fuel and jet fuel prices are going to remain volatile.

My take → Keep an eye on any headlines related to Russia selling diesel fuel to the United States. There is one ship on the way (see the RBI below), and let's see if more follow.

The continued risk around Iran and Russia pushed Goldman Sachs to raise its oil price forecast for the rest of this year. The firm is taking Brent crude to $105 and WTI crude to $94, and raising its average targets for next year to $88 for Brent (from $80) and $78 for WTI (from $73). Let's see if others follow.

While earnings have U.S. analysts swamped, Goldman's European team isn't too busy to update its "European Conviction List" of stocks. Some of the names it likes most are energy-related, including German power producer RWE (RWE-DE) and the better-known Siemens Energy (ENR-DE).

Concerns about AI, power, and rising interest rates have recently hit both stocks. RWE is down 4% this quarter, with Siemens Energy falling over 10%. Goldman remains bullish. The firm has a €212 price target on Siemens Energy and a €75 target on power producer RWE. That implies about a 30% return on RWE and a huge 70% upside on Siemens Energy.

Europe may have a world of energy problems, but that doesn't mean some of its stocks can't make you money. For more, tune in to my live CNBC coverage from Italy on Thursday, November 5.

We recently spoke with Toby Rice, CEO of natural gas giant EQT (EQT). His company just made a massive deal to buy Range Resources (RRC). We spoke with Toby about the deal, energy markets, and the importance of U.S. gas to Europe.

Another important interview on energy and AI this week was with Brian Nowak of Morgan Stanley. Brian focuses on tech firms such as Amazon, but his comments about some of the AI power players - the names we mention above - are worth a listen.

This week's Inside Line is with Christoph Halser, Gas & LNG analyst at Rystad Energy.

Could the one ship carrying Russian diesel fuel bound for America… actually skip America? It might.

The vessel we've had a close eye on for a week is moving slowly in the Atlantic Ocean. So slowly that some are now wondering if the ship will actually make it to New York. Could the destination change mid-voyage? It happens with ships all the time. A new buyer steps in to buy the cargo, and the ship changes course.

It does make you wonder… what American company would buy a load of Russian diesel right now? Putting money into Putin's pocket is not the PR that any company I know would want, but stranger things have happened.

The ship is supposed to dock in New York on November 7. Following the course of this ship right now is definitely random, but interesting.

OpenAI deepens Abu Dhabi foothold as Sam Altman inks deals spanning energy, government and investment

World's energy supply buffers have been eroded, ADNOC CEO says (Reuters)

U.S. Pushes to Restore Russian Gas Sales to Europe Via Ukraine (WSJ)

Catch up with more on energy including interviews and video content from CNBC and Power Insider.

#2943. Former Arizona wing Dalen Terry signs with Thunder

#2940. Adetomiwa Adebawore signs $35 million Colts extension one week before trade deadline

The Colts have been at the center of a lot of trade deadline rumors — and moves — ahead of Nov. 10.

But one player is staying in Indianapolis a little longer.

Defensive lineman Adetomiwa Adebawore has agreed to a two-year, $35 million extension to stay with the team through the 2028 season, NFL Network reported Tuesday.

Adebawore, 25, had been slated to hit free agency in March.

The deal, according to NFL Network, includes a $13 million signing bonus.

Indianapolis Colts defensive tackle Adetomiwa Adebawore reacts after a sack against the Los Angeles Rams at SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, Calif., Sept. 28, 2025. MediaNews Group via Getty Images

In what has been a trying year for the Colts, Adebawore is having what is projected to be a career season.

He's already matched a career high with eight sacks and has 19 tackles — four of which have been for a loss.

Adebawore ranks 12th out of 139 interior defensive linemen this season, according to Pro Football Focus' grading methodology.

The Northwestern product has racked up 10.5 sacks with 67 tackles across his first four seasons since getting selected in the sixth round of the 2023 draft.

Adetomiwa Adebawore of the Indianapolis Colts looks on prior to an NFL preseason football game against the New England Patriots in Foxborough, Mass., Aug. 13, 2026. Getty Images

#2939. AI-generated 'slopaganda' in political ads puts internet platforms on alert

The midterm election cycle has been dominated by "slopaganda," or campaign ads featuring AI-generated clips and images, creating another challenge for Instagram, YouTube and X as they attempt to monitor their platforms.

The term slopaganda dates back to early last year, when a group of experts on artificial intelligence used it in a paper to describe a new phenomenon combining generative AI slop with propaganda and misinformation.

There's no comprehensive federal law governing the use or disclosure of AI in political ads. And while there are some 30 state laws with different requirements about AI use and disclosure, candidates don't always abide by them. On the leading internet sites run by Meta, Google and Elon Musk's SpaceX, users run into AI-generated ads and AI-generated posts shared by politicians, alongside authentic videos and clips.

More than $187 million has been spent on nearly 300 ads generated or enhanced by AI, according to an analysis released last week by the Wesleyan Media Project. That's almost certainly an undercount, the organization says, because so many ads don't contain disclaimers and because determining AI use is difficult in some instances. Over 90% of the spending came from Republican candidates, a Republican party committee or a Republican-leaning group, the report found, and most don't include disclosures.

CNBC found a number of AI-generated ads running online without disclosures, including a fake video of New York Democratic Gov. Kathy Hochul that was posted by her opponent, Republican challenger Bruce Blakeman.

In Texas, Republican senatorial candidate Ken Paxton created an AI-generated video showing Democratic opponent James Talarico making controversial statements, with a three-second disclosure flashing in the middle. It's being shared as organic content on YouTube and X by Paxton's campaign. "Organic content" refers to free, unpaid posts shared on a profile or channel.

The Blakeman and Paxton campaigns didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

YouTube told CNBC that the company applies "prominent labels to realistic AI content."

"For organic content on YouTube, political candidates can request removal where their face appears to be altered or generated by AI," the company said. "For advertisers, we require disclosure when an election ad contains synthetic or digitally altered people or events to provide viewers with transparency."

YouTube parent Google also requires political ads to complete its election ads verification process and include "paid for by" disclosures.

X says advertisers must comply with applicable laws, putting the responsibility on the advertiser, not the platform. The company didn't respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Meta referred to its guidelines on political ads, including restrictions on their use in the week prior to the election. The company also mandates "paid for by" labels and maintains an ad library of political ads. In June, the company ramped up its labeling, adding a new "about this ad" tool for ads that Meta detects have been created with third-party AI tools.

One risk the tech platforms face with generative AI is being asked to create misleading images and videos. The Brennan Center for Justice tested ChatGPT, Gemini, Grok, Claude, Perplexity and DeepSeek for their willingness to facilitate deceptive content. The study from August found that many models may avoid generating content around familiar conspiracies, but can generally be manipulated into creating misleading material.

A retest of those platforms in October found that ChatGPT had made progress. It had started redirecting prompts for harmful election images into illustrations so they look like comics, or refusing to take action on them altogether.

A number of national politicians are trying to address the issue.

The Protect Elections from Deceptive AI Act, backed by senators from both parties, would prohibit knowingly distributing materially deceptive AI-generated video or audio about federal candidates. Other bills are being led by Democrats in Congress, including the AI Ads Act, which is looking to expand current bans on campaign misrepresentation to include generative AI content.

None of the proposed bills have advanced out of committee.

WATCH: AI challenge is lack of compute in the marketplace

#2935. Rockets top NBA power rankings for the week

The Houston Rockets are off to a red-hot start, leading the NBA with a 7-1 record, as well as holding wins over the Boston Celtics, Oklahoma City Thunder, and San Antonio Spurs. The team has looked like the same hard-nosed defensive team we have grown used to, but this time with some idea of an offensive plan. In addition, the team is playing within the flow of the offense, not forcing things to Alperen Sengun or Kevin Durant, with the "Stand Around and Watch KD" offense of last year showing less and less of itself. Marcus Smart has been a solid addition so far, and Jabari Smith Jr. seems poised for a career year.

Granted, it's early in the season, but the Rockets look good right now, and it seems some others might agree. The Athletic released it's new series of NBA power rankings, and low and behold, who sits atop at the moment but your Houston Rockets. Here's what they had to say about our boys:

Last ranking: FourthIn the last week: W vs ATL, W at MIL, L at DAL, W vs OKCOffensive rating: 121.2 (first place)Defensive rating: 108.9 (ninth place)

Starters: Fred VanVleet, Amen Thompson, Kevin Durant, Jabari Smith Jr., Alperen ŞengünKey reserves: Marcus Smart, Tari Eason, Reed Sheppard, Clint Capela, Bogdan Bogdanović

Houston's improved depth hasn't quite made the defense special, which makes sense. The Rockets don't have great rim protection, they have a small guard in the starting lineup and their best player is 38. The Rockets tried to get away with lineups without centers in Dallas with Şengün nursing a back injury, and it's one reason they aren't undefeated anymore. But this offense has taken off, and Houston is deeper than it has been at any point under coach Ime Udoka.

So with the Rockets cruising, the Thunder hurt and the Spurs off to an up-and-down start, it's your Rockets who are currently playing the best ball in the NBA. Right behind Houston are the 5-1 Detroit Pistons and the 6-1 Philadelphia LeBrons. I think the Rockets are better than either of those teams, but time will tell. For now, enjoy the the early-season success.

#2932. Josh Jacobs needs to display more (or any) contrition

In May 2026, Packers running back Josh Jacobs crossed a line that should never be crossed. And neither he nor his representatives are acting accordingly in what should be an effort to seek redemption.

He pleaded no contest to battery of a woman. He's fighting in court to keep the video secret. He's responding to additional allegations through a strident statement from his lawyers attacking the accusers.

The most tone-deaf gesture of all came last Thursday night, when he celebrated a touchdown by pretending to wear, and to be released from, handcuffs.

His implausible explanation, which accused Prime Video of not showing enough of the celebration and attacked the media for interpreting the images with their lyin' eyes, was contradicted by the full video of the event.

That's not the way to play this. His no-contest plea is equivalent to saying, "I did it." He should be contrite. He should be apologetic. He should be seeking redemption.

The Packers continue to stand behind him, primarily because he's the best running back on the roster and they want to win games. Their reported interest in Colts running back Jonathan Taylor strongly suggests that, if the Packers could get Taylor, the support for Jacobs would immediately evaporate.

Here's the basic reality. If the Packers were to cut Jacobs, would anyone sign him? The Chiefs took plenty of heat last week for adding kicker Justin Tucker to the practice squad; they may not be inclined to give another second chance to a player whose arrival would compound the negative reaction to Tucker's arrival.

The reality, in every sport at every level, is obvious. If the player has enough skill and ability, someone will find a way to justify behavior that shouldn't be tolerated by an industry that relies so heavily on support from the public, both directly through ticket sales and TV ratings and indirectly through taxpayer funding for stadium projects.

Still, Jacobs has done himself no favors by adopting a posture of defiance. He did something no man should ever do. He's lucky he's good enough at football to not lose his job over it.

If he was at the bottom of the depth chart in Green Bay, he would have been gone a long time ago.

#2929. Why Luka Doncic is among great scorers — of all time

It's easy to overlook what Luka Dončić is doing.

He has been putting up video game numbers for so long that we're almost inured to them in the same way our olfactory senses stop responding to a constant smell.

But Giannis Antetokounmpo slapped us out of our slumber following Dončić's fourth 40-point performance in five days in the Lakers' 138-117 win over the Heat on Monday.

"Obviously he's one of the best scorers to ever play this game, if not the best," Antetokounmpo said. "Not right now. Of all time. I feel like some people are scared to say that. But it's got to be out there."

Dončić has led the league in scoring twice and is on pace to do so a third time. He's averaging a blistering 38.3 points per game, 8.3 points more than second-place Stephen Curry. Here are the points Dončić has put up in the last five games: 42, 45, 37, 41, 52.

Yet somehow we've never thought of Dončić as an all-time scorer.

While Curry is the consensus greatest shooter ever, different players have been thrown into the conversation for all-time greatest scorer, including Michael Jordan, Wilt Chamberlain and even Kevin Durant, the latter of whom is a beacon of consistency but isn't going to regularly explode for 50-plus points.

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers dribbles during a game against the Miami Heat at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

He has a career scoring average of 29.32 points, trailing only Jordan (30.12) and Chamberlain (30.07).

And Dončić is only 27 years old. He's still in his prime. He's only getting better.

Before JJ Redick became the Lakers' coach in June 2024, he said Dončić "has the best brakes in the fu–king world" on his former podcast, "The Old Man & The Three."

"What he does is there's a constant change of pace," Redick said. "He's on-balance. He's trying to get you off-balance. Once you're off-balance, he's still on-balance, he changes his pace."

In layman's terms: Dončić is impossible to guard.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic (77) drives past Miami Heat guard Klay Thompson in the second half of an NBA game in Los Angeles, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

Heat coach Erik Spoelstra knows that all too well. When asked about defending Dončić ahead of Monday's game, he pretty much intimated that it's a fool's errand.

"It's not going to be two defenders on him or even three," Spoelstra said. "You're pretty much going to go through your entire roster."

Dončić is an offensive wizard. He's a savant. He makes scoring look easy. And it's time for him to be considered among the all-time greats.

Against the Heat on Monday, Dončić had 24 points at halftime, becoming the first Laker to score at least 20 points in the first half in five straight games since Kobe Bryant in 2007.

He's averaging a historical 1.07 points per minute. The only other player who surpassed 1.0 was Chamberlain, who averaged 1.04 points per minute in 1961-62.

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers drives to the basket against the Miami Heat on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

And Dončić is scoring at an extremely efficient rate, averaging career highs in field-goal percentage (49.7) and 3-point percentage (42.7). He's also third in the league in assists with eight per game. Heck, he's even playing defense.

He's having one of the best starts in NBA history, becoming the third player to score at least 225 points through his first six games, alongside Jordan (1985-86) and Chamberlain, who did it twice in 1961-62 and 1962-63.

Jordan led the league in scoring 10 seasons and is widely considered the greatest player of all time. Chamberlain averaged 50.4 points per game during the 1961-62 season and is the only player to score 100 points in a game.

The greatest scorer of all time is not an easy debate to enter. But in the modern NBA, does anyone compare to Dončić? And historically, Dončić is already etching his name into the record books.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2928. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2927. Analyzing Oregon Ducks' series history vs. Ohio State Buckeyes

When the Oregon Ducks and Ohio State Buckeyes typically meet on the football field, the stakes are often high. The Ducks and Buckeyes have battled in the regular season and in bowl games over the years, and the eyes of the nation are often watching.

Heading to Columbus for Oregon's first road conference matchup against the Buckeyes, the stakes are not quite what many expected of two of the top-ranked teams in the preseason, but conference title and College Football Playoff implications are still on the line. The Ducks can't afford another loss with their playoff hopes already slim, while the Buckeyes enter off a loss with a backup quarterback, suddenly throwing their playoff hopes into jeopardy.

Ohio State has a strong hold on the series history over the Ducks, winning 10 of 12 matchups. That includes five wins in Columbus, where Oregon has emerged victorious just once. "The Shoe" has been a tough venue for the Ducks in the past, but both of Oregon's wins over the Buckeyes have come within the past three meetings.

Could the Ducks get back on the right side of history and start to narrow the gap in head-to-head matchups between the programs? Let's take a look back at how the matchup has unfolded over time and the history between the new budding conference rivals.

The first matchup between Ohio State and Oregon came in a famous venue: the Rose Bowl. In 1958, Ohio State won the Rose Bowl over the Ducks, 10-7, in a close battle in Pasadena, California. The Ducks had opportunities to win the game, but miscues plagued the first matchup between the programs. In the third quarter, Oregon kicker Jack Morris missed a 34-yard field goal attempt to take the lead, and in the fourth quarter, trailing by three, Ron Stover fumbled in Ohio State territory. The Ducks couldn't stage a game-winning drive and dropped the first matchup.

Surprisingly, Oregon quarterback Jack Crabtree still took home MVP honors for the Rose Bowl after throwing for 135 yards, which was a lot back then, and nearly pulling off the upset.

That began a streak of dominance for the Buckeyes in the series, as they won the first nine matchups against the Ducks. Between 1961 and 1987, Ohio State beat Oregon six more times. Only one of those games took place in Eugene, and the Buckeyes blanked the Ducks on their home field, winning 30-0 in 1967.

Many of those games were not very competitive. After the narrow victory in the Rose Bowl to begin the series, Ohio State's next seven wins against Oregon came by an average of 16.9 points. The programs met for the second time in the Rose Bowl in 2010, and the Ducks kept it close, but a second-half rally by the Buckeyes lifted them to a 26-17 victory.

Jeremiah Masoli and LeGarrette Blount both ran for touchdowns, but the defense couldn't stop Terrelle Pryor, who threw for 266 yards and two touchdowns while rushing for another 72 yards. He earned offensive MVP honors, while Oregon defensive end Kenny Rowe was crowned defensive MVP after matching a Rose Bowl record with three sacks.

The postseason matchups between the Ducks and Buckeyes continued in 2015, but this time, it was for all the marbles. In the first season of the College Football Playoff, Oregon and Ohio State met in Arlington, Texas, to play for the national championship. Led by Heisman Trophy winner Marcus Mariota, the Ducks entered as slight favorites to win their first championship after blasting Florida State, 59-20, in the semifinal round, while Ohio State upset top-seed Alabama, 42-35.

Unfortunately, the Ducks once again couldn't slow down the Ohio State offense. Ezekiel Elliott ran wild, racking up 246 yards and four touchdowns on the ground to take home the championship with a 42-20 win. Mariota threw for 333 yards and two touchdowns with one interception, but once again, the Ducks just couldn't keep up.

That loss hung over the Ducks program for some time. Oregon changed head coaches twice before meeting Ohio State again, moving on from Mark Helfrich in 2016 and hiring Willie Taggart, only to elevate Mario Cristobal to head coach the following season.

Oregon was a contender in the Pac-12 and won the conference twice under Cristobal in 2019 and 2020. The Ducks were scheduled to host Ohio State in 2020, but the COVID-19-shortened season meant the game was cancelled, while the Buckeyes went on to play in the national championship game that season.

In 2021, the Ducks and Buckeyes finally reunited in Columbus for what turned out to be one of the biggest wins in program history for Oregon. Without star defenders Kayvon Thibodeaux and Justin Flowe, the Ducks marched into Ohio Stadium and took down the CJ Stroud-led Buckeyes, 35-28. It marked the Ducks' first road victory against a top-5 team since 2011.

CJ Verdell scored three touchdowns and racked up 195 total yards, while Verone McKinley III snagged a game-sealing interception in the fourth quarter. Anthony Brown led the Ducks at quarterback and threw for 236 yards and two touchdowns. Oregon finally broke through with an unexpected win over the Buckeyes, and it began to turn the tide for the Ducks as a program.

Oregon joined the Big Ten in 2024 and instantly circled an October matchup at Autzen Stadium against Ohio State as the moment to make a statement to the conference. Dillon Gabriel accounted for 373 yards and three touchdowns in a back-and-forth 32-31 victory over the Buckeyes in the first-ever top-5 showdown in Autzen Stadium history. Evan Stewart had his best game as a Duck with seven catches for 149 yards and a score.

Ohio State quarterback Will Howard attempted to lead the Buckeyes into field goal range on the final drive. Dan Lanning infamously deployed 12 players on the field for a defensive snap to get a stop and run more clock, which eventually caused a rule change for how the clock operates late in games. On the next play, Howard scrambled and couldn't get down in time as the clock expired and fans stormed the field.

The Ducks went on to win the Big Ten that season and held the top seed in the new 12-team playoff, where they met Ohio State in the Rose Bowl for the third time. This time, the Buckeyes got their revenge, as Jeremiah Smith torched Oregon's defense for 192 yards and two touchdowns in a 41-21 blowout win that was over by halftime.

Several players from that game, including Smith, will take the field once again for the Ducks and Buckeyes this Saturday. Stewart, as well as Matayo Uiagalelei, Teitum Tuioti, Devon Jackson, Iapani Laloulu and more will seek revenge on Ohio State in their first game in "The Shoe" in Columbus.

Oregon and Ohio State's 13th matchup in series history will kick off this Saturday at 12:30 p.m. PST and will air live on CBS.

Contact/Follow @Ducks_Wire on X (formerly Twitter) and like our page on Facebook to follow ongoing coverage of Oregon Ducks news, notes and opinions.

#2926. Meta seeks to raise up to $25 billion in debt sale as AI spending jumps

Meta is seeking to raise between $20 billion and $25 billion in a bond sale on Tuesday as the company ramps up its spending on artificial intelligence infrastructure along with AI models and services.

The debt sale is expected to be two-thirds in U.S. dollars and one-third in euros, according to people familiar with the matter who asked not to be named because the details are private. Final pricing for the bond sale should come Tuesday afternoon, the sources said.

Meta declined to comment. Bloomberg News first reported on Meta's plans.

Meta's bond sale comes days after S&P Global Ratings revised the company's outlook from AA- stable to AA- negative, citing "uncertainty" about its hefty capex spending and negative free cash flow.

In its third-quarter earnings report last month, the Facebook parent revealed that its full-year capex will come in between $135 billion to $145 billion, representing a $5 billion increase to the low end of its previous guidance. Meta posted negative free cash flow of $2.12 billion for its latest quarter, joining Alphabet and Amazon in reporting a negative figure.

Meta previously raised $25 billion in an April bond sale, and said last week that it plans to raise $95 billion in an effort to fund its AI initiatives. Of that, Meta intends to first raise $35 billion in underwritten public equity offerings, and an additional $60 billion through an at-the-market program.

Meta's stock rose 2.7% on Tuesday after dropping in 12 of the prior 14 trading sessions and closing out October with its worst month in four years.

#2924. Today in Motorsport History, November 4: Räikkönen Won the 2012 Abu Dhabi Grand Prix as Vettel Charged From the Pit Lane

Kimi Räikkönen waited more than three years between Formula 1 victories, and he got the next one on this date in 2012. On November 4, the Lotus driver won the Abu Dhabi Grand Prix at Yas Marina Circuit, holding off Fernando Alonso by 0.852 seconds in a race that lost its pole-sitter, its tidy order and, for a while, Sebastian Vettel's front wing.

Kimi Räikkönen in the Lotus E20 at the 2012 Canadian Grand Prix. Illustrative image, not from Abu Dhabi. Photo: Nic Redhead / Wikimedia Commons (CC BY-SA 2.0)

Lewis Hamilton started from pole position for McLaren and led the early running, with Mark Webber alongside him on the front row. Räikkönen started fourth. Vettel, who had set the third-fastest time, was sent to the back of the grid after the FIA excluded him from qualifying for a technical-regulation breach, and Red Bull opted to start him from the pit lane and rework the car for a recovery drive.

The race did not stay orderly for long. A collision between Nico Rosberg and Narain Karthikeyan's HRT, which had slowed with a mechanical problem, brought out the first safety car. Vettel, already damaged in the opening laps, pitted for a new nose and tyres and rejoined deep in the field.

The defining moment came around lap 20. Hamilton, comfortably in front, lost power and coasted to a stop with what McLaren identified as a fuel-pressure problem. That handed the lead to Räikkönen, who never gave it back. A second safety car followed late on after Romain Grosjean, Webber and Sergio Pérez collided, ending the races of Grosjean and Webber. Räikkönen then kept Alonso more than a second behind and outside DRS range through the final laps.

Vettel's recovery was the other story. He cut through the order and passed Jenson Button for third on lap 52 at Turn 11, finishing 4.163 seconds behind the winner. Button took fourth and Pastor Maldonado fifth.

Sebastian Vettel in the Red Bull RB8 during 2012 pre-season testing in Barcelona. Illustrative image, not from Abu Dhabi. Photo: Rich Jones / Wikimedia Commons (CC BY 2.0)

Räikkönen had returned to Formula 1 in 2012 after two seasons in rallying, joining the Enstone-based Lotus team. He had been consistently fast and regularly on the podium, but a win had not come, and Abu Dhabi was his 19th career victory and his first since the 2009 Belgian Grand Prix, which he won for Ferrari. It also gave a team racing under the Lotus name its first win since Ayrton Senna won in Detroit in 1987.

Meanwhile, the championship was tightening. Vettel left Abu Dhabi leading Alonso 255 points to 245, a ten-point margin with two rounds left, while Red Bull led Ferrari 422 to 340 in the constructors' standings and needed only a small points haul at the next round in Austin to wrap up that title. Hamilton's retirement left him mathematically out of the title fight alongside Räikkönen and Webber. He would return to Austin's Circuit of the Americas many times, including his sixth-title clincher there in 2019.

For Räikkönen, the win was a statement that his return was no novelty. His relaxed, unfiltered radio message during the race, "Leave me alone, I know what to do," became one of the most quoted lines of his career and later appeared on Lotus merchandise. It added to a reputation that had already made his 2007 title-winning run in Brazil a modern F1 legend.

For Vettel, finishing third from the pit lane after a damaged front wing and a stop for a new nose showed how hard Red Bull's package and Vettel's racecraft were to beat, even on a bad day. That 2012 campaign sits inside the dominant run that continued with his lights-to-flag win at India in 2011 and ended in a fourth straight title in 2013. For Alonso, second place kept Ferrari in the fight with a car that rarely looked like the quickest on the grid.

Fernando Alonso in the Ferrari F2012 at the 2012 Canadian Grand Prix. Illustrative image, not from Abu Dhabi. Photo: Nic Redhead / Wikimedia Commons (CC BY-SA 2.0)

The result kept the 2012 championship alive through the Austin and São Paulo finales, where Vettel ultimately secured his third consecutive drivers' title ahead of Alonso. Räikkönen finished the season third in the standings and stayed with Lotus through 2013 before returning to Ferrari for 2014.

More broadly, Abu Dhabi 2012 became a textbook example of how reliability, strategy and safety cars can rewrite a race. It also carried a small regulatory lesson: after the podium interviews, the FIA reminded drivers about language on live broadcasts. And for those who like their historical what-ifs, it left a lingering one about what a clean run for Hamilton might have meant in his final season with McLaren, a tale Christian Horner's memoir revisits from the Red Bull side.

Today in Motorsport History, October 21: Räikkönen Wins the 2007 Brazilian Grand Prix and the F1 Title by One Point

Today in Motorsport History, October 30: Vettel Led Every Lap of F1's First Indian Grand Prix in 2011

Today in Motorsport History, October 27: Vettel Clinched a Fourth Title in India

Today in Motorsport History, November 3: Hamilton Clinched a Sixth Title in Austin in 2019 as Bottas Won

Sources: Formula1.com official 2012 Abu Dhabi Grand Prix result; FIA race report; FIA on Hamilton's retirement.

#2921. Can the power of Old Trafford and vibes save Michael Carrick at Man United?

Manchester United had spent some of Tuesday night hovering just above the dotted line, 24th in a 36-team group, with Azeri champions Sabah 25th and with a game in hand on them. There are ample indications these days of how the mighty have fallen.

With a triple Champions League finalist in the technical area, United were below Bodo/Glimt in the virtual standings, at risk of being leapfrogged a day later by Sabah and AEK Athens. There have been plenty of historic lows at Old Trafford of late, but at least even they cannot come 24th, let alone any lower, in the Premier League.

A spectacular comeback later and Old Trafford was engulfed by euphoria. A turnaround was powered by bricks and mortar; or the people in that famous old ground because of them, of the past deeds they had witnessed there. "I don't like to keep talking to the lads about what this place can do in history and what's gone before them too much, but it's there," said Carrick.

Old Trafford was euphoric after the remarkable comeback (Getty)

The accusation is that United are trapped by their past, spending too long talking about it and even longer hearing their former greats turned podcasters discussing it, flailing when they appoint their ex-players as managers in bids to recreate it, blinded to the evidence that the footballing world has changed.

In their time of need, Manchester United won because they were Manchester United, and occasionally still can be. They beat Roma because of their ground which, in an inconvenient detail, co-owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe wants to replace with a new stadium in what United have called a sanity project, in part because many another feel it reeks of vanity.

It hardly feels a failsafe footballing formula for an immediate renaissance, and not merely because they already play half of their games at grounds not called Old Trafford.

Sir Alex Ferguson, the architect of many a comeback, was watching and the temptation at such times is to wonder if this was Carrick's Mark Robins moment, the transformative game that alters the direction of a reign, a club, an entire era.

Alex Ferguson is still a regular presence at Old Trafford and was watching on last night (Getty)

The prosaic answer is: probably not. United have been here before. Even in a malaise that has occupied much of the last 13 years, there have been some glorious European evenings. David Moyes grasped at a lifeline when United, 2-0 down after the first leg, beat Olympiakos 3-0. He lasted barely a month longer.

With Carrick on his coaching staff, Ole Gunnar Solskjaer saw his United go 2-0 down to a Gian Piero Gasperini side, recover to defeat Atalanta 3-2 and, like Moyes, extend his tenure, but only for a few weeks. Ruben Amorim had his own crazy comeback: from 4-2 down against 10-man Lyon to 5-4 winners. He still lost the Europa League final. It was the beginning of the end.

Carrick has seemed to face the end; at least twice, with his innate calmness. He was given a stay of execution after the 3-0 home shellacking by Bournemouth, in part because sporting director Jason Wilcox and chief executive Omar Berrada seemingly did not know what to do next. After Saturday's loss at Chelsea, it felt likely Carrick would go in the international break. Now it feels plausible he is still in charge after it.

Could Michael Carrick fight on as Man United boss once more? (PA)

And yet it is also true that, until Matheus Cunha intervened against Roma, United were on course to go 10 games without a win in the same season for the first time since Carrick was born. It is also true that United could be bottom of the Premier League by the time they kick off against Aston Villa on Sunday, and then for the international break, and then for a trip to Liverpool. Theirs has been the form of a team in a relegation battle.

That United's players, flawed as the squad is, have been underachieving was illustrated in the frenetic final 35 minutes against Roma. They ended up overwhelming the side third in Serie A. After the capitulations to Como and Bournemouth, there were crucial signs they have not given up on Carrick. There had been some indications in the first half at Chelsea, before their self-destructive streak surfaced again.

Perhaps it was telling that there were boos for Lisandro Martinez, who was sent off at Stamford Bridge, when he came on against Roma. United's players were cheered at the end, but their relationship with the crowd is still fragile. Bruno Fernandes took his own step towards redemption; and yet, after the last two months, do the United faithful still trust their team?

Bruno Fernandes took a step towards redemption against Roma (Reuters)

The most positive comments about them have tended to come from Carrick; belatedly, he was repaid for a little of his faith. Yet issues do not just remain: some have been compounded. The left half of United's defence has been disastrous this season; Martinez, who has been dreadful, is suspended on Sunday but Ayden Heaven was awful on Tuesday. Midfield has been a permanent problem and Carlos Baleba, the most mobile of the four options, is now injured.

They face objectively better teams now in Villa and Liverpool. They lost their next game after Lyon and Atalanta, suggesting they were one-offs, wins that delayed sackings rather than altering the trajectory of a tenure. Maybe Carrick will carry momentum and energy into the Villa game.

The strategy might be to rely on Old Trafford and on vibes. It is scarcely a perfect recipe but, if only for one night, it worked.

#2920. The next wave of luxury real estate: marina developments with yacht parking

Marinas are becoming the next big draw for luxury real estate, as the global wealthy search for homes to park their ever-larger yachts, according to developers.

With the number of yachts surging since Covid-19, real estate developers and investors are rushing to fill the shortage of boat slips and marina communities. There are now over 6,000 superyachts over 30 meters (98 feet) around the world, and another 200 delivered every year, according to SuperYacht Times.

The growth in the size and number of yachts has created a shortage of yacht slips. And investors are rushing to fill demand. Last year, Blackstone paid $5.6 billion for Safe Harbor Marinas, which then paid $1.5 billion in August for 65 marinas and storage sites purchased from MarineMax. European private equity firms are also on a marina buying spree to fill the shortage in the Mediterranean.

Real estate developers now see marinas as a powerful magnet for ultra-wealthy buyers, especially in South Florida. Billionaire Ken Griffin is building a megayacht marina in Miami with eight megayacht slips, including one for his 308-foot yacht.

The largest and most ambitious is Related Group's $2 billion new marina development in Fort Lauderdale. Slated to break ground next year and open in 2030, the project will include a St. Regis Hotel, condos, a beach club and slips for yachts up to 350 feet long.

"Here in South Florida is there's been a scarcity of yachting destinations and marinas and slips," said Related Group's Nick Pérez. "So when you're able to combine irreplaceable real estate and a marina, you just have a winning formula."

The 40-acre development will include a St. Regis hotel with 122 rooms, a 78-residence condo tower, 106 resort residences, as well as a deepwater marina that can hold 250 boats. It will also include a sports and wellness club with a padel and tennis court, yoga lawn, fitness center and pools. And it will have its own beach club.

Fort Lauderdale has long been the yachting capital of the U.S., with a vast infrastructure of yacht repair and renovation companies, along with crew, brokers, charter experts and shipbuilder offices. An estimated 2,000 superyachts pass through Fort Lauderdale each year, according to tourism officials. Many yacht owners are looking for real estate not only for themselves but also their crew.

Pérez said the new St. Regis development will not only appeal to the yachting community but also to a growing number of international buyers who are looking for a lower-cost alternative to Palm Beach and Miami. Several buyers from Mexico and Brazil have already purchased in the building.

He said up to 30% of the buyers at the project could be international.

"Fort Lauderdale is no longer T-shirt shops and flip-flops, it's now a luxury destination, with national retailers like Rolex, Patek, Cartier," Pérez said. "When you look at Miami and Palm Beach and the growth, now it's Fort Lauderdale's time to shine."

While boat slips are the main attraction, many buyers in Fort Lauderdale are pouring in from Southern California and the Northeast to take advantage of Florida's lower taxes.

"With the taxation situation in California, we're seeing a lot of interest from Newport and the Laguna area and San Diego," Pérez said.

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

#2919. How confident are you in the Bears and in Caleb Williams moving forward?

Welcome to SB Nation Reacts, a survey of fans across the NFL. Throughout the year, we ask questions of the most plugged-in Bears fans and fans across the country.

The Chicago Bears have won three straight games in the most impressive fashion imaginable. Caleb Williams is the 1st quarterback in Bears history to lead his team to 30+ points in three consecutive games with no turnovers. The Bears also never trailed in any of these three games, and that 3-game streak of scoring 30 points while never trailing is tied for the second-longest streak in franchise history. They went four in a row three times, all in the 1940s (1941, 1942, and 1948). Those teams were led by Hall of Fame quarterback Sid Luckman, who is the only quarterback to win a league MVP for the Bears.

There was some buzz around Williams as an MVP candidate before the season, and that was amplified following the Bears' 59-point explosion in Week 1, but the hamstring injury seemed to derail that because it kept him out of the next three games.

In his weekly NFL MVP Tracker at NFL.com, Kevin Patra wrote about the historical unlikelihood of Williams winning it.

In the post NFL-AFL merger era (since 1970) in non-strike-shortened seasons, only one player has missed three games and still won the NFL MVP award: Joe Montana in 1989. Cool Joe battled through various injuries that season, missing Weeks 6, 8, and 15, and portions of others.

While a long shot, the Bears did just beat last year's Super Bowl participants in back-to-back weeks on primetime. They also have three more stand-alone games on the schedule, including a third straight primetime affair this Sunday.

Patra also explained why Montana's performance in 1989 won it that year and how Caleb could have a similar path.

Despite missing three games, Montana was the best QB on the best team in the NFL, winning the MVP vote in a landslide (62 of 70 votes). The 49ers QB overcame the lack of counting stats by leading the NFL in all the efficiency categories: 70.2 percent completion rate, a 6.7 TD% rate, 53.7% success rate, 9.1 yards per attempt average, 270.8 yards per game, and set the then-record with a 112.4 passer rating — when 100 passer ratings were uncommon.

That's Williams' path to the MVP: Be the most efficient passer on the best team.

Caleb may not hit gaudy passing yard or touchdown numbers, but if all his other numbers are among the best in the NFL, while the Bears keep winning games, how could he not be a finalist?

After their bye week, the Rams will play in the first NFL Thanksgiving Eve game against the Packers, and quarterback Matthew Stafford's wife, Kelly, isn't too happy about it.

On Tuesday, Kelly made a post on her Instagram Story, tagging the NFL and calling it out over the league's choice for the 10-day break.

Kelly Stafford blasts the NFL's choice to schedule the Rams on Thanksgiving Eve. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

"Just curious to hear the thought process behind making the Rams the only team in the NFL without a true bye week," Kelly Stafford wrote. "Because damn, after 18 years around this league, even I need a week off during the season … and I have never played a single snap."

"I'm tired just watching from the stands. Can't imagine how the guys feel."

Kelly added, "Anyway, feel free to leave that explanation right here…"

Ironically, while the Rams will have a shortened bye week, the Packers will receive some extended time off. Green Bay will go straight into its bye week after the Thanksgiving Eve game and won't take the field until Dec. 6.

According to Jim Taylor, who has a Ph.D. in psychology of sport and parenting, "Routines are one of the most important aspects of sports that athletes can develop to improve their training and competitive performances. The fundamental value of routines is that they ensure total preparation in athletes' efforts. Routines enable athletes to be completely physically, technically, tactically, and mentally ready to perform their best."

When the season ends, the Rams will be the second-most-traveled team (34,847 miles), behind their NFC West rivals, the 49ers, who will travel 38,105 miles this season.

The 2026 NFL season hasn't gotten off to the start the Rams (4-4) envisioned, as they've been dealing with injuries across the roster and just got their record back to .500 following Sunday's win over the Chargers.

Los Angeles Rams quarterback Matthew Stafford before they played the Chargers. AP Photo/Caroline Brehman

They then have back-to-back Sunday games against the 49ers and Cowboys, but then will go on the road to play against the Seahawks.
