#2879. What the Lions have said about Jack Campbell's injury

After Lions linebacker Jack Campbell was taken off the field on a backboard early in the fourth quarter against the Vikings and transported to a local hospital on Sunday, Nov. 1, the team said he was being evaluated for a neck injury. They have provided few specific medical updates since, citing respect for Campbell's family. After being off Tuesday, Lions players return to practice Wednesday, Nov. 4. Head coach Dan Campbell is scheduled to have a press conference at 1 p.m.

Here is what the Lions and Dan Campbell have said about Jack Campbell's injury:

➤ In a postgame radio interview with Lions Radio Network's Brad Galli, Dan Campbell said Jack Campbell was alert and breathing on his way off the field.

➤ In his postgame press conference, Campbell said: "Thoughts and prayers are with Jack and his family. He's being taken care of. We don't have any new information. We'll keep you updated as this goes, but we're thinking about him."

➤ At 7:08 p.m. on Sunday night, the team released this statement: "Jack Campbell is resting and will remain in a local hospital tonight, for continued evaluation and monitoring. The team will provide further updates as we have them."

➤ At 2:31 p.m. on Monday, the Lions announced: "Jack Campbell continues to rest and recover at a local hospital. Preliminary indications are positive and out of respect for Jack and his family we will not share further information at this time."

➤ At his press conference about half an hour later, Dan Campbell referred to the team's statement and said, "It's positive. So your prayers are working. Please continue to do that and we'll leave it at that. So, just continue to pray for him, but it is positive news on Jack."

➤ Asked if he could say any more on Jack Campbell's injury, the coach said, "I can't, so I'm not going to." He added, "The team knows what's going on. I've given it to the captains."

➤ At his Monday press conference, Dan Campbell also said he visited Jack Campbell and his family at the hospital on Sunday night: "It was good. It was positive. Got to see his parents, talk to his wife. And everybody's in good spirits. And it's about the recovery and it's a small setback, that's the family. They're very positive."

➤ On "Costa & Jansen with Heather" radio show on Tuesday, Dan Campbell said of Jack Campbell, "The good news is he is improving." Dan Campbell added that he's kept the team captains updated on the linebacker's status and that the idea that he is "doing better" is "good news" for the players who are concerned about his health.

#2877. AI data center stocks and Musk's SpaceX decline as Texas governor result raises risks of a slowdown

AI data center stocks came under pressure as Democrats — seen as proponents of moratoriums on the buildout — won big in midterm elections and in key technology hubs like Texas.

Markets were already anticipating the Democrats to win the House of Representatives and possibly take Senate control. But the Texas governor mansion going to Democrats was a big blow, analysts said. Democrat Gina Hinojosa, who wants to enact an immediate moratorium on data centers in the state, won over incumbent Republican Gov. Greg Abbott. According to Wells Fargo, 27% of planned data center gigawatts is in the Lone Star State.

The midterm elections will slow the pace of data center buildouts across the U.S., Dan Ives, partner at Yorkville Ives & Co., said in a note on Wednesday morning.

"There is no way to sugarcoat that Democrats taking control especially over the Senate will create some major hurdles for AI and tech legislation in the Beltway over the next 2 years," he said.

He added that the election in Texas was the "biggest worry" of the night for tech investors and the AI trade because that's where around 25%-30% of data center gigawatts and capacity will come from.

Shares of Elon Musk's SpaceX shed 7%. The Democrats' victory in Texas is a big setback for Musk, where he heavily backed Republican candidates, and where his companies Tesla and SpaceX are based. Musk spent a record of at least $474 million on the midterms and funneled $224 million into Texas alone, where Republican Senate nominee Ken Paxton and Gov. Greg Abbott lost to Democrats.

SpaceX is currently building out multiple gigawatts of data center capacity, including projects in Texas.

"Data centers in space looking pretty appealing right now," Musk said in a post on X in the early hours on Wednesday, referring to his speculative plans to build AI infrastructure off-planet.

Neoclouds CoreWeave and Iren fell by 7% and 6%, respectively. Peer Nebius Group was down 3% and Oracle shed 4.3%. Nvidia shares were slightly in the red.

Tech giants Alphabet, Meta and Microsoft mostly bucked the trend and were higher.

The Democratic win in Texas adds to existing pressure on the bull-market-leading AI trade. The Nasdaq-100 index fell into correction territory to end October as concerns mounted that the growth of data center spending by big hyperscalers and private AI juggernauts OpenAI and Anthropic was slowing.

"We believe the equity market reaction from the midterms mostly depends on the outcome of the Texas governor's race — a Dem win could potentially be a left-tail event to the AI trade, warned Ohsung Kwon, Wells Fargo's chief equity strategist, in a report this week.

Democrats won or retained the governorships in other key data center states Georgia, Pennsylvania and Ohio. Georgia represents 7% of all the planned U.S. datacenter gigawatts, while Ohio and Pennsylvania account for 5% apiece, estimates Wells Fargo.

Notable investment strategist Michael Hartnett of Bank of America warned earlier this year that Democrats winning the Senate, combined with taking the Texas governorship could lead to a "big slump" in stocks of more than 10% into year-end.

"Data centers went from being a popular economic development tool in 2024 and 2025 to something of a pariah by the time the midterm campaigns gathered steam," wrote the Washington policy team at Optimal Advisory this week. "The midterms are likely to send a message to local and state officials that there is a political upside to blocking, delaying and/or raising the cost of data centers."

#2876. Reid Keeps Apologizing For Chris Jones’ Lack Of Production

The Kansas City Chiefs have a pass rush that isn't getting home to produce sacks, and it's becoming abundantly clear that defensive tackle Chris Jones is at the heart of the problem.

The Chiefs are paying Jones a huge amount of money to produce sacks or help his teammates get them, and he's been shut out on the stat line in two straight games now, per a report from Myles Simmons of Pro Football Talk.

Those two games are losses, and that's not a coincidence, although Simmons left that part up in the air in his piece. Jones has just 13 total tackles with one tackle for loss, three QB hits, and a half-sack through seven games in 2026, and you can make a credible argument that he's the most overpaid player in the NFL.

Coach Andy Reid doesn't agree, despite the fact that the Chiefs have just eight sacks this season, which is just over one per game. Per Simmons, only the Philadelphia Eagles have a lower sack rate than Kansas City, which has been getting one on just 3.69 percent of opponent dropbacks. By contrast, Minnesota leads the league with a 12.32 percent sack rate, with Chicago at No. 2 at 9.65 percent.

The coach is known for backing his veterans during tough times, and he continues to do that with Jones. His latest version of this has Jones "giving his all" and "doing everything he can do," as he continues to focus on the double teams Jones consistently draws.

The technical explanation Reid offered had to do with rushing four lineman and trying to get home, and he threw defensive line coach Joe Cullen under the bus for being the architect of that approach.

Reid also cited the number of blocked balls as a sign that the Chiefs are "getting close," but this isn't horseshoes or hand grenades. The coach also included a comment on the trajectory of the ball being a factor in the lack of sacks, which took the art of excuse-making to a new level.

The word salad part had the Chiefs wanting to not just get "to" the quarterback, but "on" the quarterback. The semantic hair-splitting can't hide the numbers, and the glare of the spotlight on Jones is increasing with each passing week.

Fortunately for the Chiefs, the schedule could provide a temporary answer, as they play the New York Jets, who rank 26th in the league in allowing sacks, per Simmons.

#2875. 15 players to watch amid NFL trade deadline buzz

Take a look at the NFL standings. There's no sure thing this season.

Once the Los Angeles Rams got off to a rocky start, it opened things up around the league. There is no runaway team this season, and there is also a group of teams already looking ahead to 2027. That could lead to a ton of activity before the NFL's trade deadline, which is on Nov. 10. There has been plenty of action well ahead of the deadline, with players like guard Quenton Nelson going to Seattle, defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons landing in Chicago, defensive tackle DeForest Buckner heading to San Francisco, cornerback Joey Porter Jr. being shipped to Dallas and receiver Marvin Harrison Jr. being dealt to Houston.

That's already a lot of big trades and we're just getting started. Here are the 15 names to watch between now and the deadline:

There was a quarterback change in Arizona on Sunday and it will stick. Brissett was removed late in the third quarter for Gardner Minshew, who led a wild comeback win over the Cowboys in overtime. The Cardinals announced Monday that Minshew will start Week 9 with rookie Carson Beck being the backup.

All of a sudden, the Cardinals have a third quarterback who could be moved. Minshew can handle the starting job as well as the struggling Brissett was doing, and Beck will get starts at some point. Teams don't usually trade their backup quarterback, but Brissett isn't even the backup anymore and is expendable. And he has plenty of starting experience, though his $15.5 million salary for this season would have to be managed to swing a deal with most teams. He's the most likely quarterback to move at the deadline for any team desperate for a stopgap starter (perhaps the Ravens?). – Frank Schwab

If we take the Browns at their reported word, Sanders is not likely to be traded at the deadline. You can never say never until the deadline passes, but for now it appears Cleveland will continue to carry four quarterbacks, which includes Sanders. He has been QB2 behind Deshaun Watson and has replaced Watson for a handful of snaps after Watson got hurt. Watson was sacked eight times Sunday, and the 5-3 Browns might fear a more serious Watson injury as they sit just outside the current playoff picture after Week 8's defeat to the Steelers.

Given his cost-controlled salary, low draft pedigree and Cleveland's crowded QB room, Sanders might normally be considered a higher-likelihood trade candidate. So far there has been little apparent traction. We'll see if that changes over the next week. Perhaps there's even a world where Sanders is in the mix to start in Cleveland if the free-agent-to-be Watson is not brought back in 2027. – Eric Edholm

The Eagles reportedly won't trade Barkley. That also doesn't make much sense. Philadelphia is going nowhere, Barkley is turning 30 years old in February, his production has declined since 2024 and the Eagles have never been overly sentimental about player moves. Barkley brought great memories and was the superstar on a Super Bowl champion, but the Eagles should listen to calls. They could pass if teams try to get Barkley cheap, given his current injuries, but it seems like he'd have more value to a contending team this season than the Eagles going forward.

Maybe the Eagles are serious and Barkley is indeed off the table. That won't stop speculation. It makes a ton of sense for Philadelphia to move Barkley now. That's why we're still keeping an eye on Barkley as the deadline approaches. If they don't move him, that decision will be discussed not only at the deadline but beyond. – Frank Schwab

It would be a surprise if Taylor isn't traded, after the Colts sent off guard Quenton Nelson and defensive tackle DeForest Buckner. The question is who lands Taylor and his 8,208 career rushing yards. He's still one of the NFL's best runners and plenty of teams have a need. The Giants, Broncos, Packers and Vikings top the list. While other backs are likely available, nobody else would pop like Taylor. Other players might be more prudent from a football aspect (Jeffery Simmons to the Bears, most notably) but no player acquisition would generate more buzz than nabbing Taylor. – Frank Schwab

Pollard has said he doesn't want to be traded, and the Titans reportedly have rebuffed offers, but a 1-7 record and a reasonable proposal could change their minds. He's in the final year of a three-year deal, makes fairly modest starting RB money and would provide the Titans with some salary cap relief if they moved him.

Pollard has averaged a robust 4.5 yards per carry, has back-to-back 100-yard rushing outings (and five TDs in two games) and remains in his relative prime at age 29 with fewer than 2,000 career touches. On paper, he checks every box for a sale candidate if the Titans want to add more draft assets before the deadline. And for teams looking for RB help, he might come far cheaper than some of the other players mentioned above. – Eric Edholm

The memories of Thomas' rookie season might last forever. They could lead to some team thinking it can revive his career. Thomas was a first-round draft pick in 2024 and had 1,282 yards and 10 touchdowns. He looked like a future star. In the season and a half since he has fewer than 1,000 yards. He has fallen well out of the Jaguars' hierarchy at receiver, leading to plenty of trade speculation. The Jaguars, who look like Super Bowl contenders, could keep Thomas just in case there are injuries at receiver. But teams like the Seahawks, Chiefs or Commanders would likely be interested, hoping Thomas rediscovers his rookie form in a new situation. – Frank Schwab

Interestingly, Jennings was rumored to be a target of the 49ers, trying to bring him back into the fold, but declined the offer, saying he didn't want to go back to San Francisco. That's especially interesting considering his best production came with the 49ers, notching a 975-yard season in 2024 and a nine-TD campaign a year ago before leaving on a one-year deal in Minnesota. Jennings has been almost a ghost with the Vikings, with only six catches in his six games and only modest upticks in his activity lately.

The Vikings need all the offense they can find, but Jennings hasn't been a great fit. If they can include him in a deal for a running back or defensive depth, the Vikings might consider it. – Eric Edholm

Downs has requested a trade, saying he's "open to whatever" when it comes to moving on from the Colts. The team has already kick-started its offloading efforts with Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner, and don't appear to have strong intentions to keep Downs. The 25-year-old receiver is in the final year of his rookie deal and has stepped up as the Colts' leading option with Alec Pierce hurt. Downs doesn't fit a WR1 profile but could be a quality complement for a team seeking an upgrade to its unit, similar to the Seahawks' deadline trade for Rashid Shaheed last year.

Downs is a smaller slot receiver and might be a fit for only certain teams, but with his trade request and the seeming league-wide need for WR help, he figures to be a candidate who possibly moves on soon. – Eric Edholm

Jeudy has always been mercurial. He came into the NFL as a first-round draft pick and was fine early on with the Broncos, but wore out his welcome and was traded to the Browns. With the Browns, he had a stellar 2024, with 1,229 yards and a Pro Bowl nod. But he has dipped again and with the Browns turning toward rookies Denzel Boston and KC Concepcion, Jeudy has just 171 yards this season. Still, Jeudy has one of the biggest plays of the Browns' 5-3 start, a 33-yard catch against the Ravens to set up a game-winning field goal.

Jeudy, 27, could be an intriguing gamble for a team that needs juice at receiver. He's no sure thing, but the talent is there. – Frank Schwab

Mack isn't the same player he was when he was a first-team All-Pro three times in four years. His peak might lead him to the Pro Football Hall of Fame. He's 35 years old now, but still is an effective player. And with the 0-7 Chargers going nowhere, it would make sense for the team to move on and allow Mack a chance at playoff success in what might be the final season of his career. Mack is in his 13th season and has never been part of a playoff win.

Mack would add leadership and a strong edge defender down the stretch for a contender. Mack has 8.5 sacks in 19 games over the past two seasons, including three in seven games this season. Given his age, he might not cost that much. A reunion with the Bears seems like the perfect fit. – Frank Schwab

When the Eagles drafted Smith in 2023, draft experts lost their minds. They gave the Eagles a consensus grade point average of 4.12 — better than an A. While the Eagles' first pick, defensive tackle Jalen Carter, worked out very well, Smith has not.

Smith has just 14.5 career sacks but still has the draft pedigree and enticing athleticism. The Eagles picked up Smith's fifth-year option for 2027, which means they won't be looking to give him away. But for a floundering Eagles team, the idea of recouping some value on Smith during his fourth lukewarm season has to be a possibility.

There are a lot of Eagles on this list. Philadelphia becomes the seller to watch as the trade deadline approaches. For some contender looking for a shot on a sleeper, taking a stab at Smith at age 25 could pay off nicely. – Frank Schwab

Sweat reportedly requested a trade in August, and the Cardinals very publicly said they had no interest in honoring that request. Could they have a different mindset now? Arizona has won two of three and is a fairly respectable 3-5, albeit in a very tough division the Cardinals are unlikely to win. Sweat's play has not been as impactful as a year ago, when he notched 12 sacks and four forced fumbles, but he had a huge sack in overtime of Dak Prescott in Dallas on Sunday.

Speaking of Dallas, the Cowboys figure to be among the teams that could have interest in Sweat, along with the Packers, where Jonathan Gannon (his former coach) is defensive coordinator. Sweat's contract kicks up to $23.6 million salary cap hits each of the next two seasons, which puts him outside the top-10 edge rusher deals, but there have been rumors of Sweat wanting a new deal after this season, which could complicate things. – Eric Edholm

There are conflicting reports on the 25-year-old Ojomo and whether the Eagles might consider dangling him. Normally, they'd want to build around an emerging defensive tackle who has started the past three games and been more productive recently than highly paid DTs Jalen Carter and Jordan Davis. But Ojomo is set to hit free agency, and it's unlikely the Eagles can afford him after what they paid Carter and Davis. If the Eagles have designs on re-signing Ojomo, they'll have to be creative. They are 2-6, losers of six straight, including back-to-back thumpings in prime time, and QB Jalen Hurts (along with others) is on injured reserve. The Jeffery Simmons trade to Chicago had to be an eye-opener for Philly, value-wise, and might cause the team to consider moving him before the deadline. – Eric Edholm

Baker has been tremendous for the Cardinals over his 10 seasons with the team, making eight Pro Bowls. But it might be time for a rebuilding club to move on. Baker can provide playmaking at secondary, as he has for a decade. He is signed through 2027, with a reasonable salary of a little more than $16 million for next season. So he wouldn't be a short-term rental for a team.

It would sting for the Cardinals to move on from Baker, one of the team's best players and only positive lights in the post-Larry Fitzgerald era. But it's prudent for a team looking for the future. And it would be a big get for a contending club that needs a difference maker in the secondary. – Frank Schwab

Following his three-pick game Sunday against Joe Burrow, Hooker is the NFL's INT leader with five, and his 114 return yards off those picks also is first. The 28-year-old veteran has quietly carved out a nice career over eight seasons with the Titans and is enjoying one of his better years, tackling and covering well. The Titans signed him to an extension last summer through the 2028 season with a fairly reasonable salary outside the top 10 safeties.

Given their 1-7 record at the bye, the Titans could offload more vets after trading Jeffery Simmons. – Eric Edholm

#2874. Private companies added 86,000 jobs in October, slightly higher than expected, ADP says

Private sector job growth held steady in October, with gains spread across sectors, as the labor market showed fresh signs of stabilization, ADP reported Wednesday.

The payrolls processing firm said companies added 86,000 jobs during the month, the same as the downwardly revised total for September and slightly ahead of the Dow Jones estimate for 75,000.

Employment gains came mainly in the service sector.

Trade, transportation and utilities led with 39,000 new jobs, while education and health services added 28,000, and leisure and hospitality contributed 19,000. Other sectors showing gains included financial activities (15,000), construction (13,000) and other services (10,000).

However, some of the gains were offset by a decline of 27,000 in professional and business services and a loss of 8,000 in natural resources and mining. Information services, a sector potentially impacted by the artificial intelligence buildout, lost 2,000 jobs.

Salaries continued to show gains, particularly for those changing jobs — those workers saw a 4.8% annual gain. A report Tuesday from the Bureau of Labor Statistics showed that the number of people quitting their job, a sign of labor market mobility, hit its highest level since March 2025.

Workers overall saw a 3.2% base pay gain, while gross pay increased 4.7% annually. Pay for those staying in their jobs rose 3%.

"Employers of all sizes were hiring last month, and our data suggests that job-changers were lured by a combination of bonuses, overtime, and more hours in addition to higher base pay," said ADP's chief economist, Nela Richardson. "Taken together, earnings growth for these workers hit its fastest pace since March 2024."

Companies with between 50 and 499 workers added 33,000 jobs. Large businesses added 24,000, while small firms increased rolls by 28,000.

The ADP report serves as a precursor to the BLS nonfarm payrolls count to be released Friday.

Economists surveyed by Dow Jones are looking for the BLS report for October to show a gain of 59,000 after September's modest growth of 29,000.

#2873. Ravens starting undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano at QB for ‘Thursday Night Football’

In what's been a roller-coaster season for the Ravens, they're going with a largely untested rookie under center on Thursday night.

With injuries to two-time MVP Lamar Jackson and backup Tyler Huntley, head coach Jesse Minter decided on Monday that rookie Joe Fagnano will get the start against the Jaguars.

Jackson went down on the first offensive snap in Sunday's loss to the Bills with a thumb injury, and Huntley later exited with an injury of his own.

Baltimore Ravens quarterback Joe Fagnano (12) throws a pass as Buffalo Bills linebacker Terrel Bernard (8) attempts a tackle on Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, in Orchard Park, N.Y. Getty Images

That thrust Fagnano, an undrafted rookie out of UConn, into the quarterback role. He went 10-for-21 passing with 91 yards and an interception while taking three sacks in relief.

Baltimore, earlier Tuesday, signed QB Kedon Slovis off the Packers' practice squad and added him to the active roster. Austin Reed was added to Baltimore's practice squad as the de facto third signal-caller.

Fagnano was also banged up during the loss to Buffalo and wore a brace on his ankle at Tuesday's practice, ESPN reported.

Nonetheless, Fagnano is thrilled to be getting his first start as a professional.

"It's special," Fagnano told reporters. "It's really the only way I can think about it."

Baltimore Ravens quarterback Joe Fagnano (12) speaks at a press conference after an NFL football game against the Buffalo Bills in Orchard Park, N.Y., Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026. AP Photo/Sue Ogrocki

Minter said he believes in Fagnano's composure under center and that he can handle the pressure of going against a 5-2 Jacksonville squad.

"He's cool, calm and collected; doesn't really get rattled, has a really good demeanor, played in a bunch of games in college. So, you certainly feel good about it," Minter said.

"I thought we operated really well when he was in there in the preseason, just from time on the play clock and penalties and substitutions. So, I am excited to see him go out there Thursday."

Fagnano, as UConn's full-time starter last season, threw for 3,448 yards and 28 touchdowns, both of which led independent schools.

#2872. Uber beats on revenue even as mobility and delivery units miss estimates

Uber reported revenue for the third quarter that topped analysts' estimates, but the company missed expectations in its two key businesses.

Here's how the company did versus analysts' estimates compiled by LSEG:

Revenue increased 10% from $13.47 billion a year earlier. The company reported net income of $512 million, down 92% from a year earlier, when the company had a $4.9 billion benefit from a tax valuation release.

Uber's core mobility unit, which is its ride-hailing service, saw a 2% decline in revenue to $7.52 billion, missing the average estimate of $7.71 billion, according to StreetAccount. Revenue in the food delivery business increased 23% to $5.49 billion, trailing the $5.51 billion average estimate.

Total bookings of $59.82 billion were about in line with the $59.83 billion average analyst estimate, according to StreetAccount.

Uber's revenue beat was due to growth in its freight division. Sales jumped 34% to $1.76 billion, topping the StreetAccount consensus of $1.49 billion,

Uber CEO Dara Khosrowshahi said, in prepared remarks ahead of a scheduled call, that the company's core mobility segment performed slightly below expectations overseas.

"Elevated fuel prices and tighter driver availability weighed on supply growth in several international markets," he said, adding that Uber is making "targeted investments to strengthen driver supply."

The company plans to have over 2,000 automated vehicles available on its platform by the end of 2027, half operating in the U.S. and half internationally. It expects to have around 700 available on its platform by the end of the year, adding Amazon's Zoox and Motional robotaxis to its platform in Las Vegas this quarter.

Uber is also growing its delivery business through acquisitions of Delivery Hero in Germany and ezCater in the U.S.

In its Uber Eats business, a recently expanded partnership with Costco in the U.S. is "tracking ahead of expectations," Khosrowshahi said. The company said Costco is now one of its top merchants in terms of gross bookings.

For the fourth quarter, Uber sees overall bookings of $64.5 billion at the mid-point of its forecast, in line with the average StreetAccount estimate of $64.26 billion.

The Celtics could be without both Jayson Tatum and Payton Pritchard when they face the Milwaukee Bucks on Wednesday night. Tatum, who hasn't missed a game this season, is listed as questionable with rest. Pritchard, who also hasn't missed a game, is questionable with a right shoulder contusion.

Both Tatum and Pritchard played on both nights of the Celtics Sunday-Monday back-to-back against the Orlando Magic and Houston Rockets. Paul George and Mitchell Robinson, who each sat out one night of the road trip, are both available to play on Wednesday.

Pritchard has mostly struggled offensively, with the exception of two big-time games against the Chicago Bulls; he's averaging 13.4 points and 3.4 assists, while shooting 40.3% from the field and 31% from three.

The Celtics utilized a starting lineup of Derrick White, Jordan Walsh, Paul George, Jayson Tatum, and Mitchell Robinson for the first five games of the season. #2869. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2868. Jake Ferguson gets good prognosis on ankle injury

Dallas Cowboys tight end Jake Ferguson (ankle) suffered an ankle injury in the Week 8 game against the Arizona Cardinals. While he left the game early, and he missed the rest of the game, head coach Brian Schottenheimer said the initial feedback was good.

"We'll see how he moves around. It might be later in the week that he goes, but we feel he tried to come back and just had strength to do it," Schottenheimer said. "But we feel like the results of the exams today were positive."

#2867. SpaceX signs deal with Qualcomm to provide network technology for U.S. mobile service

SpaceX announced a deal with chipmaker Qualcomm to provide key network technology for Elon Musk's Starlink Mobile service, a challenger to Verizon, AT&T and T-Mobile.

Qualcomm said on Wednesday that the terrestrial network it will help SpaceX build will "complement Starlink's satellite capabilities," with a planned rollout to begin next year. Financial terms of the multiyear deal weren't disclosed.

"Together with SpaceX, we are building world-leading cellular infrastructure in the United States, advancing 5G today, with an opportunity to lay the foundation for AI-native 6G in the years ahead," Durga Malladi, an executive vice president at Qualcomm, said in the statement.

Starlink is a critical piece of SpaceX's business, accounting for 36% of the company's revenue in the third quarter and 85% of its operating profit. But to date, the company's consumer business has been limited when it comes to mobile direct-to-device services as its satellite network can't provide all the voice, video and data coverage that competitors offer on popular smartphones.

Now SpaceX is snapping up spectrum and forging partnerships to take on the major mobile carriers. Last month, the company announced an agreement to purchase a nationwide spectrum portfolio in the 800 MHz band. The news sent shares of U.S. carriers reeling. Earlier this week, the Washington Post reported that SpaceX is in talks to acquire EchoStar's Boost Mobile.

SpaceX went public in June in the largest IPO on record and is now valued at close to $2 trillion.

With Qualcomm, SpaceX will be using the chipmaker's Dragonwing cellular infrastructure hardware and software in base stations to enable high-capacity coverage for Starlink Mobile.

Qualcomm has a long history of making chips for cellular networks, but is best known for making the modems and radios that go inside devices, not base stations for running a wireless network. The company has targeted carriers and other infrastructure companies since it made a bigger push into the 5G infrastructure market in 2020.

SpaceX represents one of Qualcomm's highest-profile customers or partners in 5G infrastructure, following earlier deals with Verizon, Dish, and Europe's Vodafone.

While SpaceX plans to use Qualcomm's technology to establish tens of thousands of new cell sites, it hasn't announced partners among cell tower companies or said how many new towers may be required.

Mobile carriers generally lease space from companies such as American Tower and Crown Castle, which commonly host multiple carriers from one site.

SpaceX did not respond to CNBC's request for additional information.

-- CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed reporting.

#2866. When the game is on the line, where has Devin Booker gone?

There was an unfortunate trend that began to manifest itself during the back half of last season. When the going gets tough, your best players need to get going. And unfortunately for the Phoenix Suns, that trend involved Devin Booker's struggles to perform in clutch moments.

From February 1 onward, in 11 clutch games in which Booker played, the Suns went 3-8. During those moments, Booker shot 6-of-20 from the field, recorded five assists against eight turnovers, and posted a -34 plus/minus.

That's concerning, especially when you consider that over his previous 10 seasons, which included some pretty horrible Suns teams, Booker owned a -2 +/- in 316 clutch situations. He shot 40% from the field during those moments and maintained a 1.8 assist-to-turnover ratio.

And it wasn't as if Booker had struggled in the clutch throughout the entirety of last season. Through the first 19 games in which he played, the Suns were 11-8 in clutch situations. Booker shot 51.2% from the field, recorded 10 assists against only three turnovers, and posted a +27 plus/minus. But upon returning from an ankle injury on February 7, the nosedive began.

You can make the argument that it's supposed to be tough, that your best player is naturally going to be the focal point of opposing defenses, and therefore his performance will be hindered by the schemes designed to stop him. During that same timeframe, from February 1 onward, Booker ranked dead last in plus/minus among players who attempted at least 20 shots in clutch situations. Jalen Brunson, Luka Doncic, and Shai Gilgeous-Alexander were at the top of the list, with Brunson posting a +41. And there were plenty of other names ahead of Booker, including Jamal Murray, CJ McCollum, Brandon Ingram, and Desmond Bane.

That only adds to the concern about who Devin Booker is and what he represents at this point in his career, particularly within the context of this Suns team. Because you can make excuses all day long about how he's being double-teamed or triple-teamed, but great players make the necessary adjustments that lead to team success. That's why plus/minus, when evaluated over an extended period, carries considerably more value than looking at the number from a single game. The trend we witnessed last season was concerning.

If double teams are being thrown at Devin Booker, why is he turning the ball over so frequently? Does he not see them coming? You know as well as I do that many of those turnovers come from Booker predictably driving into a crowd of defenders and trying to force the issue rather than relying on the finesse that's been a hallmark of his career.

Why do I bring this up? Because on Monday night against the Golden State Warriors, that familiar trend once again manifested itself. Booker's inability to execute in clutch moments ultimately contributed to another Suns loss. Yes, there are multiple variables involved, including other players who failed to perform when it mattered most. But none of those guys are making $57 million a year.

Booker re-entered the game with the Suns leading 106-104 and 4:48 remaining. A minute later, he connected on a nine-foot jumper, accounting for his only two points in clutch time. From there, things unraveled.

With Phoenix trailing 109-108, Booker missed a wide-open 12-foot jumper. On the following possession, he turned the ball over. After Jalen Green committed a turnover on the next trip down the floor, Booker was called for an offensive foul, his sixth and final foul of the game.

His final clutch stat line? In 3.8 minutes, Booker shot 1-of-2 from the field, grabbed one rebound, committed two turnovers, and posted a -7 plus/minus.

It's not the end of the world, but it does raise concerns that Devin Booker is picking up right where he left off last season, when his ability to impose his will on a game appeared to be a fading trait.

You might look at this as a one-game sample, but I see it as a sample of 11 previous clutch-time games. Perhaps that's still not enough to definitively identify a trend, especially when you consider the body of work Booker has put together over what is now his 12th NBA season. But it's the manner in which these struggles recur, time and again, that has become the focal point of frustration for a fan base desperate for success and watching a team that can ill afford to lose.

Booker's game has become predictable, and his execution in these moments has been subpar. And I know that somewhere, somebody is preparing to show me a collection of all the game winners Booker has made throughout his career. I appreciate every one of those moments and what they've meant to us as Suns fans. In the same breath, hitting a game-winner against Oklahoma City last season doesn't equate to a victory last night against Golden State.

Devin Booker has earned the benefit of the doubt throughout his career, but that doesn't mean he's exempt from scrutiny when the results begin telling a different story. The Suns need their best player to be their best player when possessions matter most. That requires better decisions, cleaner execution, and an ability to recognize what opposing defenses are taking away. Booker's past has established what he's capable of. His present is beginning to raise legitimate questions about his ability to consistently deliver it.

#2865. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2864. 5 midterm election takeaways: Tech billionaires suffer as Democrats drub Republicans

Democrats decisively took control of Congress Tuesday night, delivering a stinging rebuke to President Donald Trump and his economic record as voters voiced deep frustration with rising prices, government corruption and the AI data center build-out.

The party scored an extraordinary pair of victories in traditionally Republican Texas. James Talarico defeated Ken Paxton, becoming the first Democrat elected to the U.S. Senate from the state in nearly four decades. Gina Hinojosa defeated three-term Gov. Greg Abbott, making her the first Democratic governor of Texas since Ann Richards left office in 1995. Texas became the clearest measure of how far the blue wave reached: Democrats swept the state's marquee races in a Republican stronghold long considered beyond their grasp.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The victories were part of a broader wave of Democratic gains. In South Carolina, Democrat Annie Andrews upset Darline Graham in a race Republicans had expected to win. In Ohio, Sherrod Brown defeated Republican Sen. Jon Husted. Democrats also flipped Senate seats in Iowa and North Carolina and won key races in Georgia and New Hampshire.

The results marked a striking collapse for Trump's candidates. Paxton, Graham and Husted were among the president's prominent picks who lost after he made dozens of endorsements.

The election also sharpened the political fight over data centers and artificial intelligence infrastructure. Texas is a major center of planned data-center construction, and Hinojosa campaigned for a pause on new projects until the state adopts tougher rules. Her victory gives Democrats more influence over Texas' approach to the build-out, though the state Legislature remains in Republican hands.

The results also delivered a blow to the tech billionaires and corporate interest groups that poured hundreds of millions of dollars into backing Republican candidates who might be friendly to the massive AI data center projects they hope to build around the country. Elon Musk, the world's richest man and the cycle's largest individual political donor, spent record sums in a failed effort to help Republicans retain power, including in Texas.

Musk's spending may have become a liability as voters raised concerns about government corruption and the growing influence of billionaires in exit polls. Talarico repeatedly attacked Musk on the campaign trail, accusing him of trying to buy "his very own personal United States Senator."

"Tonight's results are a wake-up call for corporate special interests and billionaires who have been rigging the economy," said Rep. Chris Pappas, who defeated former Republican Sen. John Sununu to win an open Senate seat in New Hampshire.

The outcome is poised to reshape the technology sector's relationship with Washington. A more adversarial Congress is likely to intensify scrutiny of tech companies and the AI data center build-out. Markets reacted as the scale of Democratic gains became clear: Dow futures fell and S&P 500 futures slipped Tuesday evening.

Economic frustration was also central to Democratic gains. Many voters blamed Trump's tariffs and the war with Iran for worsening their economic situation, while the cost of living remained a persistent strain.

Democratic control of both chambers gives the party a powerful check on Trump in the final two years of his term, including the ability to block parts of his agenda and intensify congressional oversight. The results sent a clear warning: promises to bring down costs did not overcome voters' frustration with prices, corruption and the country's direction.

Silicon Valley spent record sums trying to shape the election. Instead, it got a Democratic House promising to haul its CEOs before congressional investigators.

Many of the winners ran against not just Trump, but the billionaire donors who helped bankroll Republicans. In North Carolina, Cooper told supporters that North Carolinians "will always matter more than the billionaires and the insiders."

In Texas, Talarico defeated Paxton despite more than $200 million from SpaceX CEO Musk to stop him. On the same ballot, Hinojosa defeated Abbott after blaming him for the rapid buildout of data centers across Texas, saying the governor for putting Big Tech interests ahead of local communities.

The GOP had a commanding advantage among the election's biggest donors. According to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission records, 14 of the 20 largest donor groups backed Republicans exclusively or nearly so. Crypto, AI, and online gambling interests poured more than $377 million into races.

Not all of Silicon Valley's money ended up on the losing side. LinkedIn co-founder Reid Hoffman, one of Democrats' biggest donors, cast the results as "an overwhelming wave against Trump and his cronies" and took a direct shot at Musk.

"We should get money out of politics," Hoffman wrote on X late Tuesday. "And while people like Elon are spending outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power, those of us who believe in the ideals of America cannot yield."

With control of the House comes the power to launch investigations, issue subpoenas and examine the Trump administration and its allies. That's going to be a challenge for tech companies to navigate as they find themselves in Democratic crosshairs.

Democrats are already calling for AI executives to testify under oath.

Among the first to seize on the results was Rep. Greg Casar, the Texas Democrat who chairs the Congressional Progressive Caucus.

"The House Democratic majority must make it a top priority to protect Americans' jobs, freedom, and lives from AI billionaires," Casar said in a statement after Democrats were projected to win the House. "AI company CEOs should be called to testify under oath."

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., the presumptive next chair of the oversight committee, put it more simply: "Lawyer up," he said in a statement.

The results also present headwinds for the infrastructure undergirding AI.

The Texas results appear to be a warning sign for the AI industry in a state projected to overtake Virginia as the world's biggest data center market by 2030, according to JLL. Abbott had long courted data center development before shifting his position this year as the backlash grew. Talarico and Hinojosa both seized on that anger, winning over Republican voters fed up with data centers going up in their communities. Hinojosa has promised to force lawmakers to regulate the industry.

In a CNBC Voter Poll, 56% of voters said data centers were a crucial or major factor in their decision, with Democrats ranking the issue higher than Republicans.

Trump personally pushed states to redistrict their congressional seats mid-decade to cement a Republican majority in the House. The effort largely flopped.

Many of the GOP's redrawn districts ended up falling to Democrats, despite the unprecedented effort to defend or flip them by redrawing boundaries mid-decade.

That includes seats in Texas, Florida and North Carolina, which Democrats won despite Republicans making them safer. In Texas, where Republicans began their redistricting war that then spread to other states, Democrats ended up winning most of the gerrymandered seats.

The redistricting push was always risky because no recent census was available when the maps were drawn.

Trump rolled out the red carpet for Paxton, only for Republicans to lose a Senate seat in Texas for the first time in over three decades.

Talarico's victory in Texas came after Trump endorsed Paxton over the incumbent Sen. John Cornyn, R-Texas, who held the seat for decades. In endorsing Paxton, Trump said Cornyn had not been sufficiently loyal.

Paxton prevailed in a bitter primary, and Trump's move to tip the scales spurned Senate Republicans, who saw Cornyn as a better and less expensive candidate than Paxton — who had a laundry list of scandals that plagued his campaign.

Sen. Darline Graham, R-S.C., who Trump handpicked to replace her late brother Lindsey Graham, fell to Democrat Annie Andrews. And Republican Michael Whatley lost to former Gov. Roy Cooper in North Carolina.

Democrats linked voter concerns over the cost of living to corruption in government in the lead-up to their colossal victory. The mix of the two helped propel corruption to be a top voter issue rather than a downballot concern.

Public polling indicated before Tuesday night that the issue of corruption was landing with voters. CNBC's All-America Survey conducted in mid-October found corruption was voters' top concern. Exit polls on Tuesday also tied the two issues: 69% said benefits flow to the wealthy in CNBC's Voter Poll.

The issue of corruption has exploded into public view during the second Trump term, as reports of Trump family business deals and the president's own ballooning wealth while in office dominate headlines.

In victory speeches across the country, Democrats made clear they are committed to battling a "rigged" system — a line reminiscent of once-radical Bernie Sanders presidential campaigns.

"We must get big money out of our politics, so no billionaire or trillionaire can buy our elections. We must unrig this economic system that only benefits the top one percent,"  Talarico said. "The real fight in this country is not left versus right. It is top versus bottom."

#2863. Buyers, sellers or fence-sitters? All 32 NFL teams slotted before the trade deadline

NFC East: Cowboys | Giants | Eagles | Commanders NFC North: Bears | Lions | Packers | Vikings NFC South: Falcons | Panthers | Saints | Buccaneers NFC West: Cardinals | Rams | 49ers | Seahawks

AFC East: Bills | Dolphins | Patriots | Jets AFC North: Ravens | Bengals | Browns | Steelers AFC South: Texans | Colts | Jaguars | Titans AFC West: Broncos | Chiefs | Raiders | Chargers

Where does your team sit as the NFL's trade deadline fast approaches?

Not all needy teams will swing trades prior to Nov. 10, but some certainly will look. On the other end of the spectrum, not all out-of-contention teams will hold fire sales, but with the recent spate of big names moving, there are opportunities to sell now and cash in later.

And, as we know, some teams will sit idly by as the deadline comes and goes. The NFL might be as active, trade-wise, as it has ever been in-season, but not every team is willing to pay the asking prices they're hearing.

Here's how we view all 32 teams, slotting each of them into one of three categories — buyers, fence-sitters and sellers — prior to the trade deadline. Teams are roughly ordered within each section by their perceived fits: strongest at the top, weakest at the bottom.

The Bills are the top seed in the AFC in spite of a defense that clearly needs help. Sunday's blowout of the Ravens notwithstanding, that unit isn't as likely to perform to that level against top-tiered quarterbacks and offenses. There are questions about how much the Bills want to give up in any prospective trade, and they're hamstrung a bit right now by salary-cap space (less than $4 million), but that can be rectified if needed. Safety might be the biggest position of need, although they also could look for extra pass-rush help.

Defensive tackle DaVon Hamilton suffered a torn quadriceps in Sunday's win over the Colts and will miss the rest of the season. It's hard to understate how big a blow this is. Head coach Liam Coen said the Jags will take a committee approach to replacing Hamilton, but finding a nose tackle is easier than finding other positions, so it would not be surprising to see them try to add a plugger up front. Safety is another spot they could consider. The Jaguars (5-2) are steamrolling toward a division title, and are in the hunt for the AFC's top seed. There's no better time to go for it.

They're already buyers after the DeForest Buckner trade, and they might not be done. They reportedly reached out to the Vikings about Jauan Jennings before they found out Jennings wasn't interested in a return. But receiver figures to be a position they consider upgrading, along with edge rusher and possibly cornerback. The 49ers have to be very cautious with Mike Evans; they've been a vastly better team with him on the field. Adding WR insurance, given all the injuries there, feels like a low-risk move that's on the table.

Their first buy of trade season, Jeffery Simmons, sunk his teeth into the Seahawks on Monday night. It's the kind of move that turned a very good team into a downright scary one. You can't blame the Bears if that's all general manager Ryan Poles gets done prior to the deadline, but the one area of concern coming out of the Seattle game had to be cornerback. Jaylon Johnson and Tyrique Stevenson both had rough nights with Malik Muhammad out, so we expect Poles to work the phones for CB help. They also could look for a pass-rush addition, with old friend Khalil Mack a name often bandied about these days. The Bears appear to be in Super Bowl mode now. They're out shopping.

The Vikings' offense could keep them from a Super Bowl, so they figure to seek help on that side of the ball — specifically a running back or an offensive guard. The run game has slowed down, and Aaron Jones Sr. has battled knee issues. The Vikings really could use more of a workhorse to complement Jones and Jordan Mason. They've also been connected to some pass rushers, scary as that sounds for opponents.

The Chiefs are stricken by limited salary cap space, but they figure to look and see what's out there. Wide receiver is the biggest area of concern — again — with Tyquan Thornton out for the season and Rashee Rice seemingly dealing with chronic soft-tissue issues. General manager Brett Veach has a decent history of smaller-to-medium deadline moves, so you can't rule anything out here. They also could mine for pass-rush help, but that might be tougher to find — and afford.

The primary need appears to be on defense, a unit that started strong but has tailed off. The Bengals could use help at multiple spots, although cornerback figures to be the most concerning, especially after watching Cam Ward pick them apart Sunday. A coverage linebacker and another pass rusher would be nice, too. On offense, if there's a need, it's at running back. They're averaging 3.7 yards per rush, with a long run of 15 yards this season.

Dallas is 3-5 yet still in the hunt for how poor the NFC East is. Team owner Jerry Jones says the Cowboys remain on the lookout for more help, and though they had been historically quiet at the trade deadline, Jones backed up his talk with last year's deadline deal for Quinnen Williams and the earlier-season trade for Joey Porter Jr. The Cowboys' defense is getting manhandled now. They badly need pass-rush help, but another corner would also be nice. Then again, so could more LB help. Jones said they won't want to flush more draft picks on a player who isn't under contract long term, but they might be desperate enough to add wherever they can.

The Patriots remain in contention at 5-3 (and a 3-0 mark in the division), but they could have concerns on both sides of the ball. On offense, they could use guard reinforcements after Alijah Vera-Tucker and Mike Onwenu landed on injured reserve. On defense, pass-rush help probably tops the list, but they also could seek LB and DB depth. Following the major A.J. Brown trade this summer, it would be surprising if the Patriots relinquished major draft assets. Any moves they make figure to be on the lower-compensation side, perhaps for a veteran's expiring contract.

Detroit fans have let general manager Brad Holmes know they want action prior to the deadline, especially after seeing the 49ers, Seahawks and Bears add starters last week. The Lions' defense is the biggest concern by far. The injuries to LBs Jack Campbell and Jimmy Rolder have left a big hole in the middle. Safety is in better shape with Brian Branch and Kerby Joseph back, but cornerback remains problematic. Pass-rush help also could be addressed. Can Holmes find the right help in the next week?

After adding Rashid Shaheed last year and Quenton Nelson this year, we know the Seahawks are open for business this time of year. Did Monday's home blowout loss to the Bears change their level of aggression? A struggling run game is an area the Seahawks could seek to help, although Zach Charbonnet's return could make them look elsewhere. They could try to add one more pass rusher and possibly another receiver for an offense that has been too JSN-dependent.

The Giants have found themselves atop the NFC East, and their first in-season trade for QB J.J. McCarthy has borne some early fruit. Could that move portend another (or others) if the Giants keep finding ways to win? Possibly. We know that John Harbaugh personally called Titans owner Amy Adams Strunk about Jeffery Simmons, but that call went to voicemail and Simmons went to Chicago. Defensive tackle figures to be the spot most likely to be addressed, but don't rule out a cheaper running back to stabilize that shakier position.

The Steelers already have been sellers, trading CB Joey Porter Jr. to the Cowboys. But sitting in first place in the AFC North in what's roundly expected to be Aaron Rodgers' final season, the Steelers might try to get back to buying. Not coincidentally, they could use a cornerback. Safety and wide receiver also could be spots they seek to upgrade. This doesn't feel like a team, however, that's prepared to go broke for that proverbial one final move.

They were close to being labeled "fence-sitters" after Sunday's breakout from RB Jonah Coleman. Running back had been the position that seemed most in need of being addressed prior to Coleman's big game Sunday. Now that feels like a luxury. Their most likely target position, if they try to swing a deal, figures to be offensive line or tight end.

This felt like an all-in season at one point, but that was before Myles Garrett and Davante Adams got hurt and the team started 2-4. The Rams have since evened their record at .500, but several tough games remain. The Rams certainly could be active, and another receiver certainly would help, but they've already depleted their trade assets significantly with the Garrett deal and don't have a ton to give up. They might want to add some help, but can the Rams realistically make an impact trade in time? It's unclear.

They've had some recent tailwind as a team, they've gotten Josh Jacobs back and Micah Parsons' return appears to be on the horizon. They also have some possible needs, including cornerback, if they can find the right trade partner. On the other hand, they've just been so historically quiet around the deadline under GM Brian Gutekunst (they actually have been more deadline sellers than anything), that I wonder if they'll do much.

The Texans have already bought, adding WR Marvin Harrison Jr., but is more coming? There are questions in the offensive and defensive backfields, and they continue to struggle to get the offensive line fully right. All those spots are possible. But the Texans are 2-5 and falling farther behind the first-place Jaguars. If Houston makes a move, expect it to be more on the minor end.

How seriously are the Browns taking their 5-3 start, especially after the humbling loss to first-place Pittsburgh? There are plenty of areas they could address, ample draft assets to part with and a general manager in Andrew Berry who has been among the most active in-season traders. But if the Browns don't feel this team has a legitimate chance to make playoff noise, they might be wise to keep their assets and load up more in the offseason. Browns fans would be giddy at a deadline deal, but patience might be preferred.

The Raiders feel like they are in no-man's land. They pulled back to .500 after losing four straight, but is Vegas capable of contending? The big drama now feels like when the Raiders will move off of Kirk Cousins and give the reins to Fernando Mendoza. There might be a case for the Raiders as sellers, with Cousins perhaps an intriguing piece for a team such as the Ravens that recently lost its starting QB. Other possible trade figures include Jackson Powers-Johnson and Adam Butler, but it also feels like the Raiders could ride things out without any major transactions the rest of the way.

The Panthers are a strange team to handicap. Carolina has clear needs, but at 3-4, with two straight losses, is it in position to add help? Oh, and did we mention that record puts the Panthers in first place? The playoffs are still very much a possibility, but this team being one or two moves away from being a Super Bowl contender is a stretch. If the Panthers add somewhere, cornerback feels like the most likely spot. Anything they do likely would have to be more than just a short-term rental at retail price.

Most of the NFC South profiles similarly to Carolina. There's a real shot at winning the division, but at what cost? There's also the chance the Bucs could look to sell, with names such as Yaya Diaby, A'Shawn Robinson and Chris Godwin Jr. being floated. But GM Jason Licht has not made an in-season trade since Tom Brady was QB, and it feels like the chances are greater that the Bucs do nothing.

They're 3-5 and 1-2 in the division, yet Atlanta has enough explosive potential offensively to be potential buyers. Even after Michael Penix Jr.'s four-INT game, the Falcons might think they have a real shot of taking the division. The receiver depth is light, and there are still holes to patch on defense, although they might treat the return of James Pearce Jr. from suspension as their big pre-deadline addition.

I'd put the Saints more in the "sellers" bin, sitting at 2-5 with team owner Gayle Benson already having aired her grievances with the team's performance publicly. A loss to the Browns this weekend might push them closer to moving some pieces. The question is about what players might be available and sought by other teams. If another team wanted Alvin Kamara or Cameron Jordan, the Saints would almost have to sell. Otherwise, there just aren't a lot of tradable assets that might tempt other teams.

The Jets certainly could be sellers, with the season again slipping away and the focus continuing to shift to what could be one of the franchise's biggest offseasons, armed with three first-round draft picks — with all three currently in the top 10. Adding to that should be a consideration. Garrett Wilson and Breece Hall are two big names that could be on the move, but to this point they've publicly pushed back against dealing either. If they are active, I expect lower-profile pieces such as Harrison Phillips or Nahshon Wright to be more likely than the bigger names.

The Commanders kept their season alive in the watered-down NFC East with a win over the Eagles on Sunday night, and there's a chance they could be lighter buyers if they can find a running back who fits their short- and long-term vision. They also could be sellers, with pass rusher K'Lavon Chaisson the most logical piece to try to move. It's hard to get a clear read on what GM Adam Peters wants to do, but he said he's talked to all 31 teams and continues to work the phones.

The Colts have already sold off two major assets in Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner. There's not much stopping them from continuing to put other players up for sale. WR Josh Downs has reportedly requested a trade and figures to be in play in the coming days. RB Jonathan Taylor might cost too much to be moved before the deadline, but the Colts are acting like a team just waiting for a bigger offer to pull the trigger. Lacking a first-round pick in 2027, the Colts need all the draft-pick help they can get.

For years, GM Howie Roseman has been about the most aggressive talent hunter out there come deadline time. This season, following a string of poor developments and six straight losses, the Eagles look far more like sellers on the outside. There is no shortage of players the Eagles could move. They have several expiring-contract candidates, including TE Dallas Goedert, CB Riq Woolen, WR Marquise Brown, DT Moro Ojomo and others. There also are players under contract beyond 2026 who could be available, including OG Landon Dickerson and Edge Nolan Smith. They've already made one trade and could be open for more business with this season apparently shot.

The Chargers have continued to hoard assets for now, but as the league's final winless team, it's worth wondering what they're waiting for. GM Joe Hortiz was turning away calls at one point because the team believed a turnaround was coming. It was not. If they finally become sellers, Khalil Mack figures to be an easy piece to move — a veteran pass rusher on an expiring deal. Teams seeking nose tackle help should probably call on Dalvin Tomlinson. WR Quentin Johnston also could be available if the team doesn't think he can reach his ceiling there. There are deals to be made if the Chargers shift their thinking.

The Dolphins are not making the playoffs this year, although it's reasonable to be encouraged by their competitiveness. Still, this is a sell-off phase for the Dolphins if they can cook up some interest in players such as Zach Sieler, Austin Jackson, Josh Uche or others. The problem is that Miami's roster is already so heavily loaded with rookies and minimum-salary players that there are only so many assets to move. But the Dolphins gladly will vacuum up any draft picks other teams are willing to fork over in this multiyear rebuild.

Shipping out well-liked Jeffery Simmons was a sign that the Titans are in sell-now mode. RB Tony Pollard is probably their most tradable piece, but it's not clear if they want to move him. Anyone can have WR Calvin Ridley for a song, if they want. Edge Jermaine Johnson II also might be a name you hear this week after picking him up from the Jets this offseason. This might not be a fire sale, per se, but they're apparently open for business.

Even after Sunday's dramatic win in Dallas, the Cardinals feel more like sellers than buyers, having already shipped out Marvin Harrison Jr. and sitting at fourth place in one of the toughest divisions. If a QB-needy team wants Jacoby Brissett, the team shouldn't hesitate trading him, especially after benching him for Gardner Minshew in the dramatic comeback. Pass rusher Josh Sweat feels like the biggest player who most reasonably could be moved now, although Budda Baker's name has also come up. They have some assets that could be moved, but will the Cardinals act on that?

In a single moment, when Lamar Jackson suffered his thumb injury against the Bills, the Ravens might have gone from buyers to sellers. The one exception might be at QB, where the Ravens could opt to swing a trade, be it for someone like Kirk Cousins or perhaps Bears backup Tyson Bagent, who played last year with Ravens OC Declan Doyle. But if the Ravens lose Thursday, they might consider moving a few players, including Marlon Humphrey, Mark Andrews and Roquan Smith.
